# Changelog

All notable changes to this project are documented in this file. The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/).

Versions are two-part and pre-1.0: breaking changes may land in any of them until 1.0, and each is marked **Breaking** below. But we intend to keep the configuration schema, the command-line interface of each script, and the format of the sidecar CSV files stable in each version.

## [Unreleased]

### Breaking

- **Every CSV a run writes for a person starts with a byte-order mark**, which Excel needs to open it as UTF-8: without one it reads the file in the computer's code page, and an en dash shows as "â€“" and a minus sign as "âˆ’", as a sidecar written without the mark showed. That's every report, `table-headers-sample.csv`, the unpackers' `unpack-report.csv`, and the CNX unpacker's `table-captions.csv` and `cnx-table-summaries.csv`; and `util/shortdoi.py` writes the sidecar it fills in with one. A script that reads a report with Python's `csv` module as `utf-8` finds the mark before its first column's name (`\ufeffImage`); read it as `utf-8-sig`, which drops it. Every sidecar is made UTF-8 with the mark too, so that each is read and written alike: one without the mark is given it, one of ASCII alone as much as one that holds a character outside ASCII, which Excel needs it to open as UTF-8, the run saying so, showing such a file's lines, and changing nothing else; so a script that reads a sidecar as `utf-8` finds the mark in every one (read it as `utf-8-sig`); and one Excel saved as plain "CSV" is now rewritten as UTF-8 (under Fixed). A sidecar the run can't rewrite (read-only, with another link to it, or in a folder that can't be written), or another user's, which it leaves to its owner, is read all the same, as written, and left as it is, the run saying why and what would let it rewrite one, in a warning for another user's that nothing but whose it is keeps the run from rewriting, of ASCII alone too, where one in a code page had stopped the run and one in UTF-8 without the mark was left without a word; a run as root that can't give a rewritten sidecar its owner, as in a rootless container, leaves it as it is too, said to be another user's, as is one of a user outside the container that root there may not write; and one that's there but can't be read is gone without, the run saying so, where one the user may not read had stopped the run with a traceback, a pipe had kept it waiting for good, and a link to nothing at a sidecar's own name had been passed over without a word (a setting naming one still stops the run). One that can't be read for another reason, a disk's error say, stops the run rather than make a book without it. A rewritten sidecar keeps its user attributes, access control list, and SELinux label on Linux. `util/shortdoi.py` writes the sidecar in its place, as before, keeping its owner and mode, but keeps a copy of it first, under the next free name where an earlier run's `bare-links.csv.bak` is there (which it had written over), and stops before looking anything up when it can't write the sidecar or keep the copy; a write that fails after all gives the file its original back where it can, and prints the shortDOIs found. [Sidecars](docs/sidecars.md#sidecar-files).
- **A PDF target needs LaTeX 2026-06-01 or later**, up from 2025-11-01, and stops on an older one, saying which release it found and that `tlmgr update --self --all` brings an earlier TeX Live 2026 to it. Measured with the suites under TeX Live 2025's LaTeX (2025-11-01), 14 checks failed that 2026-06-01 passes: a PDF/UA-1 book stopped (`math/setup` has no `mathml-MS` there, the key for Office's attribute), and so did a LaTeX book using enumitem's settings, built tagged; a table cell's formulas got no MathML, and an `eqnarray*`'s no file; figures' tags were left at the end of the document (`float/here` is newer), though the run said they were in place; the enumerate package's labels repeated ("(a) First part (a) Second part"); and a theorem's heading lost its period. A deck's PDF already needed 2026-06-01. [Installation](docs/installation.md#for-a-pdf-target-lualatex).
- **The book is named in `project.yaml`, and a first run writes `project-sample.yaml`.** A run with nothing naming the book stops at packaging, as it did, but writes `project-sample.yaml`, every project setting with its description, the contents the run worked out among them, to finish and rename to `project.yaml`, which both halves read; it had written `packaging-sample.yaml` with the project inline, which only packaging reads, so a book named that way got a cartridge with its name and an EPUB called "Untitled", identifier `book`. The run prints the commands that write the packaging and conversion samples. A review sample, of contents guessed, ordered from an outline, or taken from a master file, is `project-sample.yaml` too, except for a book named in `packaging.yaml`'s own block, which keeps getting `packaging-sample.yaml` with its block, a master's order in it. A `project:` block in `packaging.yaml` or `conversion.yaml` still names the book for that file's half alone, enough for a book that uses one half: one named in `conversion.yaml` with no `packaging.yaml` isn't packaged, and that's no error, and one named in both blocks gets a note that `project.yaml` would say it once. A `project.yaml` that leaves the identifier or the title at its default is warned about. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#samples).
- **Files that describe the book differently stop the run.** A project setting given different values in `project.yaml` and a file's own `project:` block, or in `conversion.yaml`'s block and `packaging.yaml`'s, had been a warning, the later file winning; now the run stops before anything is converted, naming both files, and `build-cartridge.py` alone stops for its two. The configuration fixture that said the later file wins says so (`06-two-files-disagreeing-stop`).
- **`contents-sample.yaml` is gone.** A LaTeX or AsciiDoc master's order, title, authors, and language go into `project-sample.yaml` instead, written as soon as the master is read, so a `--check-only` run, or one that stops partway, leaves it, and written again by the packager with an outline's order when `--toc` gives one; so do the contents a split's pieces resolve to.
- **A target that writes a book needs the book's title.** With none in `project.yaml` (or `conversion.yaml`'s own `project:` block), and `Untitled`, the default, naming nothing, an EPUB, a PDF, or a LaTeX master takes the title a master file gives (not the first of several documents `latex.main` lists), or the one page's in a book of one, and the run says so; with nothing to take it from, or with the title only in `packaging.yaml`, that target isn't written, the rest are, and the run ends in an error saying where the title goes. A PDF whose `pdf.metadata` file has a title is named by it, and a LaTeX book's PDF from its own build (`pdf.from: book`) is titled by its `\title`, as before.
- **`read-conversion-config.py --init` writes `conversion-sample.yaml`**, every setting at the value `conversion.yaml` gives it or its default, where it had written `conversion.yaml` itself, at the defaults, and refused when one existed.
- **Every PDF draws LaTeX's picture mode with paths, and needs pict2e.** A `picture` environment's lines, arrows, circles, and ovals, in a page's raw LaTeX, a deck's, or a LaTeX book's own, were drawn with glyphs of LaTeX's `line10` and `lcircle10` fonts, whose map to Unicode has only a space, in a few slopes and sizes: a circle asked for at 80 points was drawn at 40, the largest, with only a warning in LaTeX's log, and a line of a slope the fonts lack, `\line(7,3)`, stopped LaTeX. pict2e, which drew a struck-out term's strokes (below), is loaded for every PDF now, in a book's, a deck's, and a LaTeX book's tagged copy, and draws each as it's written. So a PDF needs TeX's `pict2e` package whether it strikes a term out or not, which the installation page's first `tlmgr` line installs, and when it's missing, the run's advice names it, and `picture_paths: off`, which builds a book that strikes out no term without it. A picture laid out around what LaTeX drew, an 80-point circle seen at 40, comes out as written now; `picture_paths: off` keeps LaTeX's own drawing for such a book, unless the cancel package is loaded, as Pandoc's template loads it for a struck-out term, for whose strokes pict2e is loaded either way, as before. A book's own command named as one of pict2e's, `\arc` or `\buttcap` say, is kept, and its own `\maxovalrad` in its preamble; one named as the trig package's, which pict2e loads (`\UseSin` and the rest), is replaced by trig's, or stops LaTeX where the text makes it, as where cancel loaded pict2e before. [PDF](docs/formats.md#pdf), [Conversion settings](docs/conversion-settings.md).
- **A book's PDF is set in IBM Plex**, where it was in Latin Modern, TeX's own design: its text in IBM Plex Serif, and its headings in Plex Serif's bold; sans-serif and monospaced type, code among it, in Plex Sans and Plex Mono; and its formulas in IBM Plex Math, whose letters are Plex Serif's; each unless the `pdf.metadata` file names another. A face the file names for sans-serif type, code, or formulas, and none for the text, is scaled to Plex Serif's small letters, a fifth taller than Latin Modern's, as Pandoc's template scales it to the text's. The faces are chosen for a reader with low vision: at 12 points an x is 2.18 mm tall in Plex Serif, where it's 1.82 mm in Latin Modern, under the 2 mm the UK Association for Accessible Formats gives for 12-point clear print; its thinnest strokes are two and a half times as thick as Latin Modern's; and it draws Greek, Cyrillic, and the symbols books use as text itself, where Latin Modern drew half the Greek and the symbols, and all the Cyrillic, from other faces. Its letters are wider, so a full line holds about 81 characters where it held 88, and a book is about 6 percent longer. Code is in Plex Mono at the text's size, its letters wider than Latin Modern Mono's, so a line holds 65 characters of it where it held 76, and a block of code with a longer line is set smaller to fit it (below). Where TeX lacks Plex, TeX Live's `plex` package (`tlmgr install plex` for TinyTeX or TeX Live from tug.org, or `texlive-fonts-extra` for Debian's or Ubuntu's texlive packages), the book is set in Latin Modern, as before, with a warning naming a file LaTeX couldn't find; naming Latin Modern's faces in the `pdf.metadata` file keeps them without the warning. The formulas are in Plex Math only where the file names no face for the text and none for formulas: one that names a face for the text alone gets Latin Modern Math, as before ([The faces](docs/configuration.md#the-faces)). A LaTeX book's PDF from its pages is set in them too, with the book's own page; its PDF from its own LaTeX keeps the author's faces.
- **A book's PDF is set on OpenStax's page, with 12-point type** (`bin/pdf-layout.yaml`), where it had Pandoc's 10-point type, justified, in the book class's margins: US Letter with 1 inch at the sides and 0.8 inch at the top and bottom, which OpenStax's tables and formulas were made for, type on lines 1.5 times its size apart, and the text set ragged right, and its footnotes and captions with it, a word never broken at a line's end. A full line holds about 88 characters in Latin Modern, the default's face then, counting spaces, where Pandoc's page held 76 and OpenStax's own 9-point PDFs hold 109 (81 in IBM Plex Serif, the default's face now, above); *Introduction to Sociology 3e* runs to 977 pages in Latin Modern, where it ran to 937. The file is the default and the place to read or copy the page's settings; a `pdf.metadata` file changes them a field at a time, so one written for Pandoc's page gets the default's for what it doesn't set, and `fontsize: 10pt`, `geometry: []`, `linestretch: 1`, and `ragged-right: false` give Pandoc's page back, but for the contents' wider column for page numbers ([The PDF's page](docs/configuration.md#the-pdfs-page)). A type size or a paper the file gives as a class option, `classoption: [11pt, a4paper]`, replaces the default's, and margins or line spacing the file's own `header-includes` set, loading geometry or setspace, keep the default's `geometry` or `linestretch` out, where LaTeX would have stopped on loading a package twice with different options. `ragged-right` is TextbookImprover's own setting, the right margin given all the stretch, as `\raggedright` gives it, and not `\raggedright` itself, which made a line break's two lines two paragraphs in the PDF's structure; ragged text is set without microtype's font expansion, which had drawn a ragged line's letters narrower to fit more on it. A type size the book class hasn't, `13.5pt` say, is set through KOMA-Script's scrextend, with the space between paragraphs, a list's spacing and indents, and the space above a page's first line at that size, where the class had ignored it and set 10 points. A LaTeX book's PDF from its pages keeps the book's own layout, or the class's page where its preamble gives none. The same page says what WCAG 1.4.8 (AAA) takes, which another page gives (Added, below).
- **Code written as raw HTML in a Markdown source is taken as written**, as an HTML source's is: a `<code>`, `<kbd>`, `<samp>`, `<var>`, or `<tt>`, on one line or more (`bin/markdown-reader.lua`, the reader every Markdown source is read with now). Pandoc's Markdown reader reads the text between such tags as Markdown, so `<code>open("c:\\projects")</code>` was `open(“c:\projects”)`, its quotation marks curled, `--` made a dash and `...` an ellipsis, and two backslashes one; and in a PDF the tags were dropped, and the code set as the text around it. Now it's code in every output, `<kbd>` a span of class `kbd`, as the HTML reader reads them, and a source that wrote two backslashes there for one, as Markdown would have it, gets two, as a browser shows the same HTML. One over more than a line of its paragraph is too, each line break in it a space, as a browser shows it, and in a block quote or a list's item, what Markdown takes off each line, the quotation's `>` and the indentation, left out of it. In a grid or simple table, whose columns are where its lines put them, it's taken as written with each row's words in their columns; and a link to a heading by the heading's text, or to a reference named by such an element, goes where it went. Where taking it as written would change how the page's blocks are read (a pipe table splits a row's cells at a `|` inside such an element, where the HTML reader wouldn't), the page is read as before, and the run says so; where only the elements over more than a line would, as in a multiline table, those are read as before, each in turn taken as written where it changes nothing, up to 30 on a page, and the run says how many were left. Read as before: an element in a YAML metadata block, whose strings have escapes of their own, one holding a backtick, which may be a code span's, one holding `\|`, a pipe table's escape, one over more than a line whose later line opens with a `>` that isn't one of its quotation's markers, or with a `|` or a `+`, as a table's lines do, its text as Markdown reads it, but code in a PDF too, where its tags had been dropped, and one whose closing tag comes after a blank line, in the next paragraph. [Raw HTML](docs/markdown.md#raw-html).

### Added

- **A PowerPoint deck's Two Content or Comparison slide is read as two columns** (`lib/pptxsource.py`), as a deck written in Markdown has them, each shape in the column it stands in, in reading order: the page shows the columns side by side, the Markdown target writes `:::: columns`, and a `pptx` target puts the slide on its template's Two Content layout again, where every shape had been read one after another. A slide where a shape stands across the middle, or whose reading order goes back to the left column after the right, is read in order, as is a slide on any other layout. [Converting a PowerPoint deck](docs/slides.md#converting-a-powerpoint-deck).
- **The run warns when LaTeX is newer than the newest release it's tested with**, 2026-11-01 (`latexbuild.TESTED_LATEX`), and builds with it: each release has changed how LaTeX tags a PDF, and the warning says the run's fixes to LaTeX's tagging may not hold there and to check the tagged PDFs. [Installation](docs/installation.md#for-a-pdf-target-lualatex) names the releases tested.
- **A build that a KOMA-Script class stops on `\DocumentMetadata` is named for that.** KOMA-Script 3.49.3 (October 2026) refuses `\DocumentMetadata`, which a tagged PDF begins with ("\DocumentMetadata not supported!"), where 3.49.2 built with it, and the way it names instead can't switch tagging on; the run says so, and that `pdf.from: pages` builds a LaTeX book's PDF from its converted pages, and that another class builds a PDF from the pages.
- **A LaTeX book's copy, and its PDF from its own files, tag a page in another language in its own** (`lib/texremediate.py`). Where `languages.csv`, or the page's file, puts a page in another language than the copy's, every copy gets lines in its master's preamble that set LaTeX's key for a language (`text/lang`) as LaTeX begins to read the page's `\include`-d file and set it back when the file ends, and that give every other page a file begins, and each chapter the master sets itself, the copy's language, since a heading or a paragraph with none of its own takes the one around it: an English file of sections after a French chapter would be French, and so would an English chapter after a French file that opens a part, or one the master sets in that part. LaTeX's tagging reads them, so a copy built with it has them, as does the `pdf` target's default PDF of a LaTeX book, which is the copy built so; built without it, they tag nothing. No file of the author's changes but the master. The lines are hooks LaTeX runs around reading any file, which look the file up as `\include` names it, its folder too: LaTeX's own hooks for an `\include`-d file by name don't run for one in a folder (`\include{parts/ch03}`), and its hooks for a file by name alone run for every file of that name, so another chapter's `solutions/ch03.tex` would have taken the language too. Each file is the page the conversion read it as, so of an `\include` in each of a conditional's branches, the one it didn't read is in the copy's language. A document that `\include`s nothing is in its page's language whole, as its `\DocumentMetadata`'s, or from `\begin{document}` in a copy without one. Not a group around the file, which a chapter that defines a macro a later one uses stopped LaTeX in ("Undefined control sequence"). The chapter's section, heading, and text take the language, and so does what the master sets after the file, in the chapter's section, as the pages have it; a page of the master's own text, which no file holds, is named instead, with how to have it tagged (a file of its own), and is in the copy's language. A page in the project's language is in the copy's, whatever region the preamble gives it. Measured on the suite's book of six files (the second French, an English file of sections after it, the third Spanish, a French file that opens a part, an English chapter the master sets in that part, and an English chapter after it), with a chapter its master sets German by `languages.csv`: every file's headings and paragraphs are tagged in its page's language and the master's chapters in the book's, the German one named, the copy without tagging builds with pdfLaTeX, and veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 profile passes the PDF. The copy and that PDF had been in the book's language throughout.
- **A LaTeX book's copy, and its PDF from its own files, tag each passage the book's own babel commands put in another language in it** (`lib/texremediate.py`). babel sets such a passage's hyphenation and words and tags nothing, so a screen reader had `\foreignlanguage{french}{déjà vu}`, a German quotation in `otherlanguage`, or a chapter after `\selectlanguage{french}` in the book's language, where the PDF from the pages has `\foreignlanguage`'s and `otherlanguage`'s passages in theirs, as Pandoc reads them. Every copy of a book that loads babel and uses its commands so gets lines in its master's preamble that add to hooks babel runs as it selects a language: a passage of a paragraph or more (`otherlanguage`, `otherlanguage*`, `\foreignlanguage*`, or `\selectlanguage` between paragraphs) gets LaTeX's key for a language (`text/lang`) as babel selects it, and `\foreignlanguage`'s words a Span of their own in their language, so the paragraph they open keeps its own; an `otherlanguage` or `otherlanguage*` begun inside a paragraph, as babeltags' `{\fr un mot}` is, is a Span to its group's end, or to where LaTeX's tagging ends the paragraph, a table's cell, or a list's label around it first, and what begins inside it is in its language. babeltags' commands are these. A `\selectlanguage` inside a paragraph puts what begins after the paragraph in its language. After a passage in a group, the text is in the language around it, not the one babel goes back to, and a `\selectlanguage` of a language babel is in already, its region aside, changes nothing: a page `languages.csv` puts in Spanish, babel's English throughout, is Spanish after an English quotation in it, and from its head where it opens with `\selectlanguage{english}`. A page with no language of its own, which the lines for the pages' languages (above) give the copy's, is in the one babel's commands have selected for it, before its `\include` or before a chapter the master sets, so it's in the language LaTeX sets it in, as is the master's text after a file that leaves babel in another. A language's tag is the one the reading gives babel's language in a preamble, else babel's own (`latin` is `la`); one with neither, as one `\babelprovide` makes with no locale of its own, is in the language around it, and the run names it for a PDF it builds. The run counts the commands by language, and a book whose commands all name its own language, every page in it, gets no lines. No file of the author's changes but the master, and built without tagging, nothing is tagged; pdfLaTeX builds the copy as before (TeX Live 2023 and 2026). Measured on the suite's books, built with LuaLaTeX: each passage in its language, every word where it is without the lines, to under a hundredth of a point, the structure the same but for the Spans, and veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 profile passing each. polyglossia's commands aren't tagged ([A LaTeX book as the source](docs/latex.md#the-source-target)).
- **A deck's table whose Header Row is checked over a blank first row is reported** (`pptx-table-header-row-blank`), as four of the tables in OpenStax's *Introductory Business Statistics 2e* chapter 10 deck have one over their numbers, and one in its chapter 2 deck over its labels and formulas: PowerPoint's checker counts the Header Row, which has no headers to announce. The table is in `table-headers-new.csv`, its guess made without the blank row (`none` for chapter 10's four, `first-column` for chapter 2's), and a row deciding it deletes its blank rows from the copy, so its value is the headers of the rows left. The page leaves the rows out, as it now does any table's blank rows at the top, and a Header Row checked over them marks nothing there. A row written before for a table with a blank row at the top and no Header Row checked is read this way too: `first-row` deletes the row and makes the next one the header ([Slides](docs/slides.md#the-reports-and-the-sidecars)).
- **A slides run saves a deck in ODP or PowerPoint 97-2003's format as PowerPoint, through LibreOffice, and reads that** (`lib/decksave.py`). A `.odp`, which LibreOffice Impress saves and Google Slides downloads, or a `.ppt` is saved once, beside the original, which is left as it is, and from then on the PowerPoint file is the deck, as an archive's files are the book once it's unpacked: what the run reads, what the sidecars name, and where corrections go. A deck whose PowerPoint file is beside it already isn't read, with a warning when the original is the newer; a deck that can't be saved is named with the reason, and the rest go on, an empty file, one that isn't a deck of its kind, and an ODP saved with a password named before LibreOffice sees them; `--check-only` says what it would save; without LibreOffice (`soffice`, or `LIBREOFFICE` naming it) the run says how to save each by hand. LibreOffice runs with a profile and a folder of its own, and is stopped with what it started after five minutes. What its file loses is said where it applies: an embedded object's picture, a PowerPoint 97-2003 deck's equations among them ([Slides](docs/slides.md#decks-in-other-formats)). Measured on 13 decks, about two seconds each; and of Google Slides' two downloads, the PowerPoint one serves better.
- **The CNX unpacker reports an exercise's picture the bank marks decorative** (`exercise-image-decorative`), its alt text empty, which no format gives a screen reader, on the page that shows it: the bank marks some so that a question is about, as *Biology for AP® Courses*' apbio-ch03-ex065 asks of a Western blot, "What is the relationship between the green line and the red line?" A row of `image-alt.csv` describes it, under the page's name and the picture's path, or the path alone for every page. In the 20 books here that embed them, the exercises show a picture in 3,938 places, 202 of them one marked so, 177 in *Biology for AP® Courses* (174 pictures).
- **An editor's placeholder for alt text, "Alt Text Placeholder", is reported as alt text that says nothing**, as 19 of *Organic Chemistry*'s exercise pictures have it, with "image placeholder", "placeholder image", and "placeholder text".
- **The audit reports a figure's image whose alt text is its caption, word for word** (`source-image-alt-is-caption`), as Pandoc gives a Markdown figure written `![caption](file)` with no alt text of its own: a tagged PDF says it twice, as the caption and as the picture's description, which NVDA read so, and the picture has no description of its own. A page hides such a caption from a screen reader, as Pandoc's HTML writer does, and a PDF can't, since PDF/UA-2 has every caption tagged as one and every figure described. Alt text after the image (`{alt="..."}`) settles it; a row of `image-alt.csv` describes the picture in the book's outputs, though the audit, which reads the source as it is, still reports it. A caption another rule finds, a credit or a file's name, is reported by that rule alone. [Auditing](docs/auditing.md).
- **The run warns of a LaTeX book's contents that its class sets with code of its own**, which LaTeX's tagging reaches only in part: where a book in amsart, amsbook, amsproc, memoir, or one of KOMA-Script's classes sets a contents or a list of figures or tables, for a `source` target with tagging and for the PDF target's copy, saying what LaTeX's tagging does to it in that class: the AMS classes' entries are laid out wrong, memoir's run together with their dot leaders, and KOMA-Script's are hidden from a screen reader as page decoration. A class of the book's own, beside it, that loads one of them (`\LoadClass{amsbook}`) counts as that class, for the warning and for the copy's changes to amsbook's, amsart's, and amsproc's lists. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#a-classs-own-contents).
- **A table OpenStax's math books draw with a light rule under each row is drawn so** (class `row-rules`). Their design draws a light gray rule under each row of the body of a table it classes `unstyled`, none under its head, and in the calculus books and *Algebra 1* a gray so light it's hardly seen in Key Equations; the CNX unpacker gives such a table the class `row-rules`, a Key Equations table in those books `row-rules-faint` too, and the dev-math books' and the corequisite book's tables of the class `steps`, worked examples' steps most of them, which were drawn with no rule at all. A page and an EPUB draw a rule under each row of a table's body, in OpenStax's gray (#757474), lighter in dark mode, or its lighter one (#F1F1F1), where Pandoc's stylesheet rules a table's body above and below (`page.css`). A PDF draws booktabs' rule between rows under each row of its body, across the table's columns, an artifact, where the writer rules a table above, under its head, and below; a row that a cell spanning rows reaches below isn't ruled there. A Word file draws such a table as it draws any other. [CNX input](docs/cnx-input.md), [PDF](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **`math.display_style_in_text` sets a formula in running text in display style where its page does.** Pandoc's reader drops MathML's display style, so a formula in a paragraph that its page sets in display style, a fraction's parts at full size or a sum's limits under and over it, is in text style in every output. Set to true, the run marks such a formula before Pandoc reads the page, where display style changes it (`lib/mathstyle.py`), says how many it kept, and a PDF, a page, and an EPUB set it in display style; a Word file doesn't. It's off by default, since a formula in display style makes its line taller. OpenStax's repositories hold 6,768 such formulas, and 473 in a table's cell, which it marks too, most of them in the calculus books, in English and in Spanish. [HTML input](docs/html.md).
- **A page in another language than the book's says so in every target, and `languages.csv` decides a page's language** (`lib/languages.py`, `sidecars.languages`). Each page's language is worked out once, for every target: its row in `languages.csv`, keyed on its source as `page-names.csv` is, else the language its own file declares, else the project's. A Word file's is the language most of its letters are in, as Word resolves each one's from the run, its styles, and the document's defaults, since Pandoc's reader reads no language from a Word file at all; a deck's, the language most of its runs say, else its default's or its masters'; a Markdown file's, its `lang`; an HTML page's, its `<body>`'s or its `<html>`'s, so a page the CNX unpacker writes for a module in another language than its book is in the module's; a LaTeX book's, its preamble's. A Word file or a deck whose custom property `company` is `OpenStax` is in the language its custom property `language` gives: OpenStax's Word files are Pandoc's output on a template whose default is US English, and nothing in a section of *Precálculo 2ed* said otherwise. A file's own language that differs from the book's only in region doesn't override it. A page in another language is marked so in its HTML page, the book's banner, footer, menu, and notes' heading on it marked in the book's; in the root of each of its EPUB chapter files, which Pandoc's writer gives the book's; in the listings of the book's pages, its entry in the contents page, the menu, an EPUB's contents and navigation, and the pager, and on the Notes page its notes' heading, and each of its notes moved to a page in another language; as a passage of its language in the PDF, tagged as one; in a Word, Markdown, or AsciiDoc target's file, a file `merge: groups` makes of pages in two languages holding the other page in a div of its own; and in a deck's page, PDF, PowerPoint file, and presentation. The book's packages keep the book's language. A `source` target's copy gets a row's language, or OpenStax's property's, in place of the file's own, in a Word file's default and every run and style that said its own language, a deck's default and masters and runs alike, an HTML page's `lang`, and a Markdown file's, a passage marked in a third language keeping it. The run names each page in another language and where its language came from, `languages-new.csv` drafts a row for each that its own file put there, and a row naming no page, or whose language isn't a tag, is warned about ([A page's language](docs/sidecars.md#a-pages-language)).
- **A worked example's steps written as one formula are a table whose reasons wrap**, as the CNX unpacker writes them. *College Algebra 2e* and the books written as it is set a worked example's steps as one formula, the math in columns and each step's reason in words after a wide space, or an instruction before it, which can't wrap, so a step ran past its line in a PDF and past a narrow window: in *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, by up to 224 points, and fitted at three quarters of its size, 36 points into the margin still. The unpacker lays such a formula out in a layout table (class `formula-steps`), as the dev-math books' steps are: its math cell for cell, its columns aligned as the formula aligned them, and its words text in a column of their own, which wraps; and lines labeled in words written as one formula likewise, as *Chemistry 2e* labels a cell's half-reactions. OpenStax's books have 852 such formulas, in 18 books, 160 of them in *College Algebra 2e*. A PDF sets such a table as LaTeX measures it: each column of math as wide as its widest formula, and the words on one line beside them where the table so fits its line, and otherwise wrapping in what's left of it, no narrower than their longest word or formula; where even that's too wide, its spaces narrowed and then its type set smaller, as another table's are. A formula in it doesn't break over lines. Unlike other tables, it has no rule above or below it, in a PDF, on a page, or in an EPUB, as OpenStax sets the formula, unless `table-headers.csv` makes it a table of data. Nor do the tables the dev-math books and *College Algebra with Corequisite Support 2e* lay out where they stand, most of them a worked example's steps, which the unpacker gives the class `steps`. A step OpenStax set in display style, as its table set its cells or as it set a part of the step, most often the integral sign alone, is in display style in a PDF, on a page, and in an EPUB, all of it: Pandoc's reader drops MathML's display style, and with it which part was, so the unpacker marks the formula (a span of class `displaystyle`, which `html-source.lua` makes `\displaystyle` at the start of its TeX). *Calculus Volume 2* has 91 such formulas, in 26 of its 29 tables of steps, *Calculus Volume 3* 37, and the first volume of *Fizyka dla szkół wyższych* 2; with them, *Calculus Volume 2*'s chapter 3 is 121 pages, where it was 120. A Word file sets them in text style. In *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter, where 28 formulas were fitted, two still too wide at three quarters of their size, by 36 points and 0.6, 2 formulas are, and no table of steps, 41 of its 51 at their own width; only the formula 0.6 points too wide still is. On a narrow screen a step's cells follow one another on a line, where a layout table's stack. [CNX input](docs/cnx-input.md#baked-as-openstax-bakes-it); [PDF](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **`--wcag` checks each HTML page, presentation, and EPUB's content document with axe-core too**, given to `convert.py`, `check-output.py`, or `audit.py`, in Playwright's Chromium, with the optional Playwright; axe-core 4.14.0 is in the repository (`vendor/axe-core`, unmodified, under the Mozilla Public License 2.0), and `AXE_CORE_DIR` can name another copy, which the summary notes. Its rules for WCAG's criteria run, chosen by their criteria at the version and level `--wcag-version` and `--wcag-level` choose, WCAG 2.1 AA by default (63 rules in axe-core 4.14.0; 2.0, 2.1, and 2.2 at AA or AAA), with `--best-practice` its 27 best practices, and with `--experimental` the rules among those it's still trying, which it leaves out unless asked (a large table's cells without headers, a caption written in a table's first cell, and a paragraph styled as a heading among them), each finding of one saying so; its deprecated rules never. A rule a page fails is a note, never an error (`axe:<rule>`), and so is one it couldn't decide (`axe-review:<rule>`), counting the elements and naming the first by their selectors, the criteria named and linked, and axe-core's page on the rule; with `--verbose`, each element is a note, with its markup. A presentation TextbookImprover wrote is checked slide by slide, with all its steps shown, and its own scripts running, held first to the repository's: reveal.js and its plugins byte for byte, `oer-reveal.js`, and a start of reveal.js that's settings alone; a presentation whose scripts aren't is named as not checked. A page with colors for dark mode is checked again in dark mode, without saying again what axe-core couldn't decide in light mode. A symbol in a formula, whose contrast axe-core doesn't measure since it isn't text, is left out and counted where a page's formulas take the colors of the text around them: in _Introductory Business Statistics 2e_ (OpenStax), 1,993 of the 2,083 elements axe-core couldn't decide. Each page is locked down, for the day a web front end checks pages strangers upload (`lib/lockedbrowser.py`): the check in a process of its own, off the network in a namespace of its own; Chromium's renderers in its sandbox; every request answered from the pages' folder or the EPUB, or refused, and the page kept from navigating away; the page's own scripts blocked by a Content Security Policy; and each layer it couldn't have, where the system refuses it (Chromium's sandbox can't start for root), said. Each step of opening a page, and each page, has a time limit, past which the page is named as not checked and the rest go on; a presentation's window is at most 4,096 pixels across and down, whatever its settings give its slides; and the check ends with the run that started it. A probe page of its own, checked first in each run, shows that axe-core works and the lockdown holds, and with nothing to check, `check-output.py --wcag` runs it alone, and a probe presentation, which checks the setup. Without Playwright, with one older than 1.48, or with a Chromium that doesn't start, a line says so, and the rest runs as before. Measured with two processors: 40 pages of 29 KB in 12 seconds, a second of it starting the browser. [Checking the output](docs/checking.md#axe-cores-wcag-checks).
- **`audit.py --wcag` keeps axe-core's results on each page in its cache** (`audit-cache.json`), with every file the page's check read, the page itself and what it loads, and the names of those that weren't there, and checks a page again only when one of them reads otherwise, or when axe-core, Playwright, the rules, `--verbose`, or the check itself has changed; an EPUB's content documents are kept each on its own. The summary says what wasn't checked again and since when, and with nothing to check, no browser starts: on the EPUB of _Introductory Business Statistics 2e_ (OpenStax), 218 content documents, the audit with `--wcag` took 1 minute 44 seconds, and the next 0.3 seconds, with two processors. `--force` checks every page again, and `--no-cache` neither reads nor writes the cache; a part of it that isn't what an audit wrote is started again. [Auditing](docs/auditing.md#the-report-and-when-its-stale).
- **`audit.py --wcag` keeps veraPDF's WCAG findings on each PDF in its cache too**, the rules of the WCAG profile it failed, with their failed checks, by where the PDF is and its SHA-256 (`lib/pdfcheck.py`), and gives a PDF the profile again only when the PDF has changed, or veraPDF has, by its launcher and what its `--version` says, or the Java it runs, the folder `VERAPDF_WCAG` names, by each file's SHA-256, `CLASSPATH_PREFIX`, or the check itself. A PDF made again replaces its own, and one renamed or copied is found by its SHA-256. What's reported of the rules kept is decided as a run's is, so another `--wcag-version`, `--wcag-level`, or `--verbose` needs no run of veraPDF. A run with a warning keeps nothing, nor does a PDF written again while veraPDF read it, nor a veraPDF that doesn't say which it is. The PDF's section says when it wasn't checked again, and since when. The PDF is still read every time, for its metadata and veraPDF's PDF/UA checks. The audit suite checks it with its stand-in for veraPDF, which answers `--version` now; what veraPDF's own `--version` prints was read in its source (veraPDF-apps 1.30.3), not run here. [Auditing](docs/auditing.md#the-report-and-when-its-stale).
- **`audit.py --wcag` takes its report for current when nothing has changed**, as an audit without it does, where it had made the report again each time, since what its checks find turns on what's installed too. The audit decides once its checks have run from the cache: the report is current when no file was read again, no check ran on one, and what each check's findings turn on is as it was when the report was made, the setup the check kept them with or, for a check that couldn't run, what it said, as well as the files, named as the report names them, the options, and the report's forms. So installing Ace, the modules veraPDF's WCAG checks need, or another veraPDF has the report made again, as does a check that keeps nothing, and so runs each time. On _Instrumental Analysis_'s 195 pages and EPUB, with axe-core and Ace, the audit took 349 seconds, the next 1.3 seconds, which found its report current, and one after a page had changed 3.3 seconds, axe-core checking that page alone. The audit suite checks it with its stand-ins for veraPDF and Ace, and a browser when there is one. [Auditing](docs/auditing.md#the-report-and-when-its-stale).
- **`audit.py` keeps a PDF's own checks in its cache**, what it reads of the PDF with `pypdf`, its metadata and claims, and veraPDF's verdict on those claims (`lib/pdfcheck.py`), by where the PDF is and its SHA-256, so a PDF made again replaces its own and one renamed or copied is found by its SHA-256. An unchanged PDF isn't read again, nor given veraPDF's PDF/UA checks, while `pypdf` (its version), veraPDF (its launcher, by where it is and its SHA-256, what its `--version` says, the Java it runs, and `CLASSPATH_PREFIX`), and TextbookImprover's own check are the same, where every PDF had been read and checked by each audit. Its section of the report says when it wasn't read again, and since when. Nothing is kept from a run veraPDF didn't finish, or of a PDF written again as it was read, and with a veraPDF that doesn't say which it is, nothing is kept or read. `--wcag --verbose` still reads what a PDF has where each of veraPDF's failed checks kept for it is. The audit suite checks it with its stand-in for veraPDF, counting the PDFs `pypdf` reads. [Auditing](docs/auditing.md#the-report-and-when-its-stale).
- **`--wcag` checks each EPUB with DAISY's Ace too, when it's installed**, given to `convert.py`, `check-output.py`, or `audit.py` (`lib/acecheck.py`), found through `ACE` or as `ace-puppeteer`, `ace-cli`, or `ace` on the path. Ace's own checks are reported, each a note, as axe-core's are (`ace:<rule>`): the package's language and accessibility metadata, which EPUB Accessibility 1.1 asks for, and in the content documents an `epub:type` without its ARIA role and a page break without a label, the rules Ace adds to its copy of axe-core. Its axe-core results are counted and left out, since they're the rules `--wcag` runs itself, in a browser locked down as Ace's isn't. With nothing to check, `check-output.py --wcag` runs it on a probe EPUB of its own, which checks the setup; a run it couldn't finish is a warning in Ace's words; `--quick` leaves it out; and an audit keeps each EPUB's result by where the EPUB is, with its SHA-256 and Ace's version, so an EPUB made again replaces its own and one renamed or copied is found by its SHA-256, and runs Ace again only on an EPUB that has changed, or with another Ace. Nothing is kept with an Ace that doesn't say its version, nor of an EPUB written again as Ace read it, and a part of the cache that isn't what an audit wrote is started again. Ace isn't locked down, so it's for books you made. On _Introductory Business Statistics 2e_'s EPUB, Ace 1.4.6 took 2 minutes 30 seconds, with Playwright's Chromium, and found nothing of its own. The suites use a stand-in (`tests/fakeace.py`), and read a report Ace 1.4.6 wrote. [Checking](docs/checking.md#daisys-ace), [Installation](docs/installation.md#optional-ace-by-daisy).
- **`--wcag` checks each PDF against veraPDF's WCAG 2.2 machine profile too**, given to `convert.py`, `check-output.py`, or `audit.py`, with the two modules veraPDF's release doesn't include, `wcag-validation` and `wcag-algorithms`, and the libraries they use, from the folder `VERAPDF_WCAG` names: nine jars and two profiles, downloaded, with nothing to build ([Installation](docs/installation.md#optional-verapdfs-wcag-modules)). Each of the profile's own eleven rules a PDF fails is a note, never an error (`verapdf-wcag:2.4.9-1`), counting its failed checks and naming their pages, with the criterion's number, name, and level and its Understanding document, from W3C's source of WCAG 2.2 (`lib/wcag.py`); with `--verbose`, each failed check is a note instead, at its page, with what veraPDF found, the text and the structure element when it names them, and what the PDF has there that veraPDF's report doesn't say, a link's address and the line repeated characters are on. The summary lists them after the run's verdict, under a line saying they're experimental and may be wrong. Only the rules for criteria within WCAG 2.1 AA are reported, unless `--wcag-version` and `--wcag-level` choose another version and level, and a line counts what was left out: at the default, the rule for 2.4.9 (AAA), which 789 of *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s bare links fail. Without the modules veraPDF passes those rules unchecked and says nothing of it, so a probe of the run's own, a PDF made to fail one of them, is checked beside the files, and the summary warns when the modules aren't there or didn't load, and of what else veraPDF leaves unsaid: a file it left out of its report, an error it printed as it went, a `wcag-validation` of another version than its own; `check-output.py --wcag` with nothing to check runs the probe alone. [Checking the output](docs/checking.md#verapdfs-wcag-22-checks).
- **A page for WCAG 1.4.8 (AAA)**, `bin/pdf-layout-wcag-aaa.yaml`, for a book whose PDF is to meet Visual Presentation: the default page with 12.5-point type, about 77 characters to a full line in IBM Plex Serif where the default's hold 81 (a book set in Latin Modern, whose lines hold more, needs 13.5-point type, 78 to a line where the default's hold 88, as the page's comment says), paragraphs 2.5 times the type's size apart, top to top, and footnotes and captions spaced as the text is, 1.5 times their type's size, where setspace and the caption package set them single-spaced. Its lines are the default's, 1.5 times the type's size apart, space-and-a-half as W3C's technique for the criterion measures it (C21). A book on it is about 10 percent longer: *Introduction to Sociology 3e* runs to 1,139 pages in Plex, where the default gives it 1,032 (at 13.5 points in Latin Modern, 1,208, where the default gives it 975). W3C's explanation of the criterion measures from the font's single spacing instead, 1.2 times the type's size in LaTeX; the configuration page gives that reading's three lines, which made Sociology 1,408 pages in Latin Modern. Copied into a book's folder and named as its `pdf.metadata`, it's that book's page; copied over `bin/pdf-layout.yaml`, every book's, though a book's own metadata file that sets `header-includes` replaces the page's two lines of LaTeX, as it would any setting of the page's. The page says it's meant to meet the criterion (`wcag-aaa: true`, TextbookImprover's own), so a book's build on it measures the text's full lines as LaTeX sets them, not counting a line a line break ends, and warns where they average more than 80 characters, naming the type size, to the half point above, at which they'd hold no more: a book in Latin Modern at 12.5 points holds about 84, whether TeX lacked Plex, where the warning of the fallback now says the PDF no longer meets the criterion, or the book named Latin Modern's faces. [The PDF's page](docs/configuration.md#the-pdfs-page).
- **A LibreTexts book from a web archive.** `unpack-site.py` recognizes LibreTexts' pages (the `libretexts` profile, `lib/libretexts.py`) and reads the book as the site shows it, doing from what the archive holds what the site's scripts do after a page loads. The order is the listings', from the home page down, the Front Matter's and Back Matter's pages the book's front and back matter in `contents` (`role: front` and `role: back`), and the book's title, authors, and description are the home page's, where the generic profile had read the order from a menu reaching 38 of *Instrumental Analysis*'s 199 pages and titled the book "Chemistry LibreTexts". The home page, the matter's own landing pages, and TitlePage are left out, Table of Contents is the run's own contents page (`generate: toc`), and Index and Detailed Licensing, empty pages a script draws, are made from the site's tag directory and license report. Each page's display formulas are numbered as the pages configure MathJax to (`tags: "all"`, 14.4.2 the second on page 14.4) and set as a LaTeX book's numbered formulas are, its `\ref` and `\eqref` written in the text are links to them, `\PageIndex` is the page's number, in alt text too, and mhchem's `\ce`, which texmath doesn't read, is the TeX it stands for (`lib/mhchem.py`). An Example, Exercise, or Note box, a `<section>` the run opens up, as it does any section, is a div of class `box`, which every target draws as a box (below), its legend a heading. The site's editor's mark for adding a paragraph (CKEditor's magic line), which an edit saved into a page, is taken out, where every target showed its "↵" and the EPUB fetched its icon from the web. On *Instrumental Analysis*: 194 pages in the site's own order; 450 formulas numbered or labeled and 309 references resolved, each number and reference the same as MathJax 4.1.3 gives, run on every page with its own configuration; all 268 `\ce` rewritten; 841 index entries. `unpack-site.py`'s `project.yaml` can say an entry's `role` and `type`, and a generated contents page's `name`, for any profile that knows them. [A book saved from the web](docs/site-input.md#libretexts); [Making a WARC](docs/making-warcs.md) says what to open while recording one, and what a browser's archive records about the person who made it.
- **`images.downsample` scales down an image much wider than it's shown.** A web page often holds an image larger than it shows it and leaves the browser to scale it, and every output carried it whole: 442 of *Instrumental Analysis*'s 483 images are wider in pixels than the pages show them, up to 11.9 times (a photograph 2,150 pixels wide shown at 180, 17.6 MB), 145 MB in all, and its EPUB was 142 MB. Set to a number, the most times an image may be as wide as it's shown, the run makes copies of the wider ones scaled down to that, which `html`, `epub3`, `docx`, `pdf`, `revealjs`, and `pptx` targets carry in the originals' place: an html target copies them beside its pages, and Pandoc finds them before the originals on its resource path. The originals, and the copies the source-like targets write, don't change, and a copy larger than its original isn't used. A PNG copy of a drawing in color, an original in RGB or RGBA of 4,096 colors or fewer, gets an adaptive palette of 256 colors, since a copy's smoothed edges hold thousands of colors, which compress worse than the original's few; a photograph's copy keeps its colors. The width an image is shown at is its page's, in CSS pixels (a height read with the image's proportions, a percentage of the column), and no wider than the column, 36em; a PDF draws an image no wider than its line, 6.5 inches on the default page, so 4 keeps a scaled image at 354 pixels an inch or more there. At 0, the default, nothing is scaled unless `images.compress` (below) sets a level, and the run says how many images are wider than they're shown, and by how much the widest is. Scaling needs Pillow (`sudo apt install python3-pil`), and a run that sets it without Pillow stops before reading a page. On *Instrumental Analysis* at 2: all 173 images over the limit scaled, 130.3 MB to 26.7 MB, 135 of them drawings given a palette, where without it 77 drawings' copies were larger than their originals and kept; its EPUB is 41.6 MB, its zip of Word files 47.0 MB, and its PDF 54.2 MB, where they were 142, 155, and 165. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#images-larger-than-theyre-shown).
- **`images.compress` makes a book's images smaller, at one of three levels**, for a smaller PDF, EPUB, Word file, or deck: `print`, `screen`, or `small`. Each is a limit, as `images.downsample`'s, of how many times an image may be as wide as it's shown, 4, 2, or 1, which `images.downsample`, set, gives instead; and the highest quality a JPEG keeps, 90, 85, or 75: a JPEG saved at a higher quality is written again at that one, at its own size where it isn't scaled, as is a JPEG scaled down (one saved at a lower quality keeping its own), `print` keeping its color at the resolution the original has it (4:4:4, say) where the others write it at half resolution each way (4:2:0), libjpeg's default. A JPEG's quality is read from its quantization table, as the quality libjpeg makes that table for. In a PDF on the default page, a copy keeps at least 354, 177, or 89 pixels an inch. OpenStax saved 87 of *Principles of Marketing*'s 110 photographs at quality 99 and 21 at 94, 47.9 MB of its images' 59.6; its PDF of 61.3 MB is 36.3 MB at `print`, 23.9 MB at `screen`, and 13.4 MB at `small`, and veraPDF finds nothing in each. Where no target makes them smaller, the run's warning says how many JPEGs are saved at a quality over 90, the highest, beside how many images are wider than they're shown, and names both settings. It needs Pillow, as `images.downsample` does. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#images-larger-than-theyre-shown).
- **A stand-in for Pandoc 3.9, to run the test suites as with the oldest Pandoc TextbookImprover takes** (`tests/pandoc39/`): `PATH="$PWD/tests/pandoc39:$PATH" bash tests/run-all.sh`. A script named `pandoc` that runs the Pandoc installed, 3.11 or later, with 3.9's interface, as read from 3.9's source: it says it's 3.9, stops on an option 3.9 doesn't have as 3.9 does, writes math as text unless MathML is asked for, and gives a Lua filter 3.9's functions. It's how `--math-method` and `pandoc.TableBody`, which stopped every conversion on 3.9, were found, and it's to be run before each release through 1.0; the unit suite checks that it still answers as 3.9. It can't show what 3.9's own readers and writers make. [Testing](docs/testing.md#running-as-pandoc-39-would).
- **A heading that skips a level is lifted to one below the heading it's under** (`heading_levels: outline`, the default), and what's under it with it, so a screen reader's list of headings and an EPUB's or a PDF's outline have no gap and every heading keeps its place: a heading's parent is the nearest one before it at a shallower level, and under the page's title a page's first headings are H2 at the deepest. A heading that skips nothing keeps its level, none goes deeper, and the run says how many moved. Sites skip levels for a heading's size: LibreTexts sets each box's legend as an `h5` and its Solution as an `h6` under sections that are `h2`s and `h3`s, 136 headings of *Instrumental Analysis* that the output check named, and every one of the Nu checker's 68 errors there, and DCIC sets a page's sections at the third level. `heading_levels: source` keeps the source's levels. A deck's headings, which are its slides and sections, keep theirs, and a Word file's own copy keeps its styles. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#headings-that-skip-a-level).
- **A PDF's block of code with a line too long for its line is set smaller to fit it, and wrapped where that's not enough**, where its lines ran on past the line, into the margin or off the paper, with no word from LaTeX where the code is highlighted. LaTeX measures each line of a block as it sets it, and the run after sets the whole block smaller, as much as its longest line takes, to three quarters of its size at least, the `code_wrap` setting's part of it (from 0.5 to 1); a block with a line too long for its line even at that size is set at it, and each of its lines too long for its line is wrapped where it reaches the line's end, a quotation's where it's in one, so that no code runs into the margin: broken after a space, which the line keeps, or in a word longer than the line, between two of its characters, after a run of marks where a letter or a digit follows, rather than between two letters or inside the run, where it can be, never between a letter and an accent or a point on it, and not in or right after its indentation, unless that leaves a part fewer than 20 characters; a word that fits a line whole moved to the next row whole; each part after the first set the line's indentation and two characters further in, but no further in than leaves it 20 characters, an arrow (↳) drawn in those two characters, a drawing that a copy of the code doesn't hold and that a tagged PDF marks an artifact, which a screen reader doesn't read; and a line's parts kept together on a page unless they'd take more than half of it, when a line in no language breaks across pages, and a highlighted line, set in a box as fvextra sets one, can't, and is warned of where it's too long for a page, the warning saying to break it into shorter lines in the book's files. `code_wrap: 1` wraps every line too long for its line at the code's own size. The PDF's text of such a line is the line as written, one line of code in its structure, its spaces kept. The build counts what it fitted with the tables and formulas, and says which blocks it wrapped and at what size. In the default's faces, a line holds 65 characters of code at 12 points, and 86 at three quarters of its size: of the 667 blocks of code in OpenStax's books, 119 have a longer line than 65, 74 of them in *Principles of Data Science*, and 44 one longer than 87, wrapped at the default, by a count of their lines' characters. A footnote's code is fitted to the footnote's line, a footnote's in a table's cell too; code in a table's cell is fitted with its table, which a line of it too wide for its column sets smaller, as words a no-break space joins now do, a piece TeX can't break, where it had been measured by the pieces between its spaces; a deck's PDF fits a slide's code the same way, and the `latex` target's LaTeX marks each block for LaTeX to fit, the marks taken out when it's read back as a LaTeX source. LaTeX's verbatim no longer breaks a line of code after a hyphen, as the PDF's ragged right let it. A formula in text or a numbered display still too wide runs into the right margin as far as a quarter inch from the paper's edge, where the narrower of the page's margins, the left one on a page whose right margin is wider, had set how far. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf); [Conversion settings](docs/conversion-settings.md).
- **A PDF's table or formula too wide for its line is fitted to it**, as LaTeX sets it: a table, a display formula, or a formula in text that is an array or another environment, as a worked example's steps are, none of which TeX can break. LaTeX measures each, and on the run after narrows its spaces (a table's columns half an em apart, a formula's `\qquad` and its `\mspace` wider than a quad a quad, an array's columns closer) and, where that's not enough, sets its type smaller, to three quarters of its size at least; a table or a display formula fitted is centered on the page, so one still too wide runs into both margins alike, and one that would come within a quarter inch of the paper's edge is set smaller still, to half its size at least. A numbered display isn't centered, its number at its line's end; a display under the `fleqn` class option isn't fitted; on a page of two columns, where the margin is the other column, a formula is set smaller to fit its column, to half its size at least, and the build warns of one still too wide for it; a table whose columns wrap, once fitted, has its words whole, which TeX doesn't hyphenate, and one whose spaces alone fill its line, as those of a table of 45 columns whose widths are given do, has them narrowed and is fitted the run after; a fill-in blank in a cell counts as three underscores at most when its table is measured, the shortest it's drawn to fit its line (below), so that a blank too long for its column doesn't of itself set the table in smaller type; and a table of the class `float`, which Pandoc's writer sets as a `tabular` in a `table` float, isn't fitted, since a table is measured by the widths longtable records. Whatever is too wide is fitted on LaTeX's first run, and each fit is measured again on the second and fitted again where it's off its line by more than 4 percent, or, for a formula in text or a numbered display, too wide at all, since smaller type isn't always as much narrower (Latin Modern Roman's smaller designs, and unicode-math's forms for subscripts, which in 12-point type it sets math of 10 points or less in, are wider for their size); nothing is fitted after the second run, so that the last run has the pages its contents' page numbers were read from. The build says what it fitted, names what's still wider than the text, and warns of what's too wide for the paper, or for its column, and the smallest size it's in. This had set a table whose longest words don't fit a line in `\small` or `\footnotesize`, the first they fit in by a count of their characters, which left *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s F tables, in 12-point type, up to 172 points too wide in `\footnotesize`; its appendix of tables had 40 lines too wide, and has none, each of its 13 tables fitted to the text's width, the smallest at 83 percent of its size. *College Algebra 2e*'s first chapter had 28 formulas too wide, by up to 224 points; 11 fit with their spaces narrowed and 17 in smaller type, two of those, worked examples' steps in text, still too wide at three quarters of their size, by 36 points and by 0.6. And a table of 25 columns or more, more than a line holds by that count, is left in the columns the writer gives it, for LaTeX to fit: the count had shared out a line with no room left in it, so that the writer gave the columns of a table of 45 columns of numbers less than no width, and the build named 96 of its lines too wide for their columns. A deck's PDF fits a slide's table or formula the same way, where a table of twelve columns ran 147 points off its slide and a formula 234, and the `latex` target's LaTeX is marked for LaTeX to fit as the PDF's is, the marks taken out when it's read back as a LaTeX source. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf), [Slides](docs/slides.md).
- **The PDF build names the lines wider than the text, or than their column in a table**: how many are wider by more than 3 points, in tables, display formulas, or lines of text, the widest, and the pages they're on, widest first. LaTeX reports each such line (an overfull box) in its log, which Pandoc's PDF route keeps to itself; the build reads it from Pandoc's own log of the run (`--log`, its `MakePDFInfo` messages), where the line numbers it gives are the LaTeX's, and each line's page is the one whose label comes last before it. A line of the contents, or of a list of figures or tables, is named as the list's: TeX gives it a line of the list's own file, which the build had looked up in the book's LaTeX, and *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s 18 page numbers too wide for its contents' column were counted among its lines of text, on no page. *Instrumental Analysis*'s first PDF had 75 such lines, found then by reading LaTeX's log by hand: wide tables, binary expansions written as one display, and a description list's terms, which LaTeX's tagging gives no way to wrap. Its second has 68: 42 lines in tables on 8 pages, a source's citation with no spaces in it and tables of numbers the widest, 17 display formulas, and 9 lines of its chapters' lists of sections. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A layout table, which every target writes as one.** A table that only lines things up, as a worked example's steps beside their results do, is a layout table when its page says so with `role="presentation"`, or `role="none"`, ARIA's other name for it, made `presentation` as the page is read; when a LaTeX book tags it as a division of paragraphs (`\tagpdfsetup{table/tagging=div}`); and when `table-headers.csv` says `layout`, a new value, which is how a Word table becomes one, since Word records nothing for its "Mark as layout table". A table of one cell is one whatever its source, since one cell has nothing to head (a Word file's box around a note), unless its source makes the cell a header cell (a page's `<th>`, a LaTeX longtable's head, a Markdown table's header row, a Word table's row marked to repeat as a header, but not the header row Word's table look makes of a box's, as Word makes one of every table it inserts), or a caption, its own or a label beside it ("Table 25.5"), presents it as a table, which a PDF numbers. It's asked no caption and warned of no header row, and it keeps its place in its page's count of tables, so the keys of the tables after it don't move. A head it comes with, as a Word table whose first row Word repeats as a header does, is rows like the others, with no header cell, since LaTeX's tagging takes a longtable's head for header cells even in a division of paragraphs, a TH outside any TR, which PDF/UA-2 rejects. `table-headers-new.csv` lists a page's own as `layout`, the report naming the page as the supplier, and a row naming its headers, `none` among them, makes it a data table; a single cell, or pictures alone, which the header guess counts no table, isn't listed. The pages and the EPUB write it with `role="presentation"` and no focusable scroll region, its cells stacking on a narrow screen, since WCAG 1.4.10 lets a data table keep its two dimensions and scroll but not a layout table; the PDF tags it as a division of paragraphs, a row's cells in turn, not a table (`table/tagging=div`, in a group, only where tagging is), a data table inside it as a table, and its caption, no table's, as a paragraph before it, its id an anchor there, which LaTeX numbers neither; a Markdown target writes it as HTML with its role, as the AsciiDoc target did, so it reads back as one; and the `latex` target's LaTeX reads back with it. A `source` target writes a person's `layout` into an HTML page, as its role, and into a LaTeX book, as its tagging, and says it can't into a Word file. [Sidecars](docs/sidecars.md).
- **`media.lowercase`** copies each image and file a page names under its name in lowercase, the letters A to Z, and names it so, in an html, revealjs, markdown, or asciidoc target, for a server that tells case apart, whatever case the source spelled a name in. Two files whose names differ only in case stop the run at the media gate, before anything is written, as two images that one safe name would cover already did (and two files a page links to now do too, where the second copy had replaced the first), and a file is told from another as the file system tells it, so one named in two cases on a system that ignores case is one. A `source` target's copies keep the author's references, since they name the author's own files, which the run doesn't rename. A target written into the sources' own folder, on a file system that ignores case, leaves a source's file as it's named, and the run warns of it.
- **A page or group in `contents` can say what it is**: `type: glossary`, `preface`, `index`, `bibliography`, `part`, and 14 more of EPUB's structural semantics (any other is warned of and ignored), for a page whose source can't say so itself, as a Word file's preface can't. A type isn't inherited, since a chapter isn't a glossary because its Key Terms page is one, and a top-level entry with no `role` is in the part of the book its type implies: a book-level glossary is back matter, unnumbered, in the PDF and the EPUB alike. A type on a page a split cut, or on a group's opening page, is the group's. A LaTeX book's sample gives the bibliography page the run writes `type: bibliography`, at the top level after any parts and appendices. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md).
- **An EPUB says what part of the book each page is.** Each chapter file's body is front matter, body matter, or back matter as its top-level entry's `role` in `contents` says, where Pandoc's writer had called every one body matter (all 11 of GIAM's, its copyright page and bibliography among them); a page inside a chapter is in the chapter's. A page's type, from `contents` or from its heading (Pandoc's own `{epub:type=preface}`, or a class such as `{.glossary}`), is on its section with the DPUB-ARIA role (`doc-preface`), named by its heading, since NVDA takes the role for a landmark and lists one with no name as a blank line; a page in an appendix is an appendix, and a LaTeX book's bibliography a bibliography. The landmarks, which held only the title page, name the generated contents page, the start of the body matter, and the book's bibliography, glossary, or index. A Markdown target puts a page's type from its heading back on the heading. [Building an EPUB](docs/epub.md#what-goes-in-and-in-what-order).
- **A setting an earlier version had says where it went**, where the run said only that it was unknown: `images.spacer_log` is `reports.spacer_images`, v0.1's `manifest` block was split between `project:` and `packaging.yaml` (and `util/migrate-config.py` moves a v0.1 configuration whole), and `reports.table_headers_missing` went with its report in v0.3. And a key that's another block's setting says whose: `version` under a conversion's `defaults:` is a `packaging.yaml` setting, `title` there a project one.
- **The output check finds a formula shown as its TeX** (`formula-shown-as-tex`), one Pandoc couldn't convert to MathML: Pandoc says so only in a warning among the run's output, so a run could end "nothing found" with `$r_{s} = \left( …$` on a page, as the statistics book's geometric mean was. The finding's detail is the formula. A page with a script is left alone, since the script may be what renders it.
- **The output check finds a formula Pandoc kept as its text** (`formula-kept-as-text`), one whose MathML texmath can't read, a script or a fraction of the wrong number of parts or an element it doesn't know, which Pandoc's HTML reader keeps as its text in a span of class `math`, the parts run together (`x2` for *x*²), without a warning: the output check said nothing of 20 such formulas on the pages of *Fizyka dla szkół wyższych*, before the CNX unpacker repaired their scripts. The finding's detail is the text. A page with a script is left alone, as for `formula-shown-as-tex`.
- **The output check finds a table with no data cell** (`table-without-data-cells`), a data table whose every cell is a header: it has no body, which an accessibility checker reports as a table missing its body content. 63 of *Instrumental Analysis*'s tables were so, in its pages and its EPUB (below), and the output check said nothing of them. The finding's detail is the table's place on the page. [Checking](docs/checking.md).
- **A guessed `contents` gives a page the type its title names**, and so does one ordered from an outline with `--toc`: an OpenStax chapter's Key Terms is `type: glossary` and its References `type: bibliography`, which an EPUB marks for a screen reader, and the preface and index are `type: preface` and `type: index`, where the guess had said `role: front` and `role: back`. Only titles with one meaning are typed (Preface, Foreword, Dedication, Epigraph, Acknowledgments, Prologue, Epilogue, Afterword, Abstract, Colophon, Copyright, Glossary, Key Terms, Bibliography, References, Works Cited, Index, and an Appendix by its first word), so an Introduction isn't. An outline's order had given the preface and index no role at all, which made them main matter, numbered with the chapters in a book with `numbering: true`.
- **`convert.py --quick`** checks the run's output without epubcheck, the Nu HTML checker, and veraPDF, as `check-output.py --quick` and `audit.py --quick` already did, and the summary says they weren't run. The built-in checks still run.
- **The suites run their cases side by side**: the convert, LaTeX, and EPUB suites a process a case, and the PDF suite its two books at once, on half the processors by default, with `-j N` for more or fewer (to `run-all.sh` or to a suite; `-j 1` runs them one at a time). A case's lines are printed whole and in order. With the suites' runs of `convert.py` passing `--quick` and the duplicates gone, every suite took 260 s on two processors, from 1,010 (419 one case at a time).
- **A folder of PowerPoint decks is slides: each deck is checked, the decisions it needs are reported, and a copy is written with the decisions made so far.** The checks are what PowerPoint's own Accessibility Checker reports (a slide with no title, an object with no alt text, a table with no header row, a section left with its default name, merged cells, reading order, media, duplicate titles and section names), plus alt text holding a file name or extension, an error since PowerPoint counts it as missing, alt text that says nothing (punctuation, "image", a clip-art id, a camera's file name, a shape's default name), alt text Office generated ("Description automatically generated", or its newer "AI-generated content may be incorrect.") or over `images.alt_max_chars`, a group or a textless shape with no alt text, a decorative shape that still has some, a picture on a master with none, a missing language or title, hidden slides, bare links, diagrams drawn from loose shapes, columns laid out with tabs, and XML PowerPoint can't read as written (an empty shape, which stops it opening a deck, or a prefix used without its namespace declared, which it repairs by removing content: Pandoc's PowerPoint writer leaves both), all in `slides-check.csv`. `image-alt-missing.csv` keys a picture on its image's content (`media/` and the first 16 characters of its SHA-256), with the image written at that path. One row then covers every copy, on every slide of every deck and on the layouts and masters, so a logo pasted onto 50 slides is marked `[decorative]` once; the row names every copy and what each has now. `deck/media/...` limits a row to one deck, and an object with no image of its own (a chart, a group) is keyed on `deck/slide-N/shape-M`, the deck's file name and ids that survive moving slides; the most particular row wins. A blank `Alt` keeps what a picture has, as it does a book's image. `table-headers-new.csv` has each table with no header row, with the table census's guess, and `slide-titles-new.csv` each slide with no title, and each whose title an earlier slide has, drafted `Title (2)`, for the new `slide-titles.csv` sidecar. A `source` target, implied as `remediated` when none is declared, writes into a copy of each deck: alt text and PowerPoint's decorative mark; header rows; a title for each untitled slide, its own heading made its title in place where it shows one (below), else a title above the slide, where a screen reader reads it and the slide doesn't show it, as PowerPoint's Add Hidden Slide Title does; a title in place of one another slide in its deck has, what it adds after the old title's last run or equation, in the last run's formatting; the project's language as the default of a deck that has none; the first slide's title in the file's properties when they have none; and what PowerPoint can't read as written put right where nothing is lost, so a deck Pandoc wrote opens: an empty shape taken out, and a prefix used without its namespace declared. The XML is edited as text, in AlternateContent's Choice and Fallback alike, and every other part is copied byte for byte. The copies are checked into `output-check.csv`. On OpenStax's 13 decks for *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*, with every alt-text row decided, the copies' check finds nothing. A slides run of PowerPoint decks needs no Pandoc unless a target converts them (below). [Slides](docs/slides.md).
- **A slide's reading order is decided in a sidecar, `reading-order.csv`**, keyed on `deck/slide-N`: the ids of the shapes to read, in order. `reading-order-new.csv` drafts an order for each slide read in another order than it's laid out, the title first and then each shape after those wholly above it or beside it and to its left, as near to that as the slide comes without drawing two overlapping shapes the other way round, since PowerPoint draws in the order a screen reader reads; `Shapes` says what each id is, and `Note` which overlapping pairs kept their order. A `source` copy puts the shapes an order names in its order, in the places they had, a group with its pieces and a shape in AlternateContent with its Fallback, and writes no order that would change which of two overlapping shapes is drawn over the other, a callout's tail, a connector's bend, and a freeform's points counted, saying why. A slide in the sidecar's order isn't counted against its copy. [Slides](docs/slides.md#the-reports-and-the-sidecars).
- **A deck's bare links and equations, as a book's.** A slides run reports each address a deck's link shows as its text in `bare-links-new.csv`, as a book's run does, and a `source` copy writes the bare-links sidecar's decisions: a Title as the link's ScreenTip, and a Replacement as its text, and as its address when it's an address. With `math.repair_equations`, each equation gets the characters it means, as a Word file's does, except that a bar written as an upper limit stays one, a macron in place of an en dash: in PowerPoint, NVDA read an equation by its characters, and of six ways of writing x̄ it read the bar of only that one. On OpenStax's decks, 2 of the 73 equations change, each an en dash set over a letter. `util/remediate.py` passes `--links` and `--repair-equations` to decks too.
- **A deck's video or audio with captions isn't reported as missing them.** PowerPoint keeps captions added with Insert Captions as a WebVTT file in the deck, listed with the clip as a caption track ([MS-PPTX] 2.1.3 and 2.2.4.1), and a track holding at least one cue now counts, as in a deck captioned in PowerPoint. A clip with no track is reported as before, and one whose track is missing, empty, or not WebVTT is reported with that said.
- **A slide's own heading becomes its title, in place, as PowerPoint's Set as Slide Title makes one.** A deck whose layouts have no title placeholder puts each slide's heading in a body placeholder or a text box, which looks like a title and isn't one, and a hidden title above it had the heading read twice. `slide-titles-new.csv` now drafts each untitled slide's title from the heading it shows, marked `TI`, `Source` naming the shape: its topmost shape holding words, when that's a shape at the top of the slide's tree, not grouped or styled by the theme, and its text a title's length; a heading an earlier slide has is numbered, `Introduction (2)`. A `source` copy given a title that's the heading's text, or that text with a number added, makes the shape the slide's title, keeping its place, position, text, and look, the number going after its last run: what its text inherited from its layout and master goes into the shape's own list style, since a title inherits the master's title style instead.
