On five slides PowerPoint's own Set as Slide Title converted, every paragraph and run came out as PowerPoint had them; and in LibreOffice, the slides of the decks measured rendered pixel for pixel as they had, but for those given a number and a text box LibreOffice reads differently as a title. Any other title is still added above the slide. The run's and `util/remediate.py`'s summaries count both. [Slides](docs/slides.md#the-copy).
- **A font whose license keeps a deck from being edited is found, and can be replaced.** A deck that embeds a font for preview and print only opens read-only where the font isn't installed (`pptx-font-restricted`), and one that uses such a font without embedding it, set to embed its fonts, won't save there (`pptx-font-unembedded`), both seen with Avenir Next, which macOS has and Windows doesn't. A slides run says how many decks use each such font and how many of them embed it or use it without embedding it, and `fonts.restricted: replace` gives a `source` target's copies another font in its place, the one `fonts.replacements` names, else the nearest the deck uses by PANOSE, with the restricted font's embedded files taken out; `fonts.replacements` replaces whatever font it names. [Slides](docs/slides.md#fonts).
- **A copy's lists of its fonts and slide titles say what it holds.** PowerPoint keeps both in the file's extended properties (`docProps/app.xml`) and writes them afresh when it saves; a copy whose fonts or titles change lists the replacement in the font's place and the titles it wrote, not the original's lists, which named a replaced font among the fonts used and "PowerPoint Presentation" for each slide that had no title. Lists named in another language, or that don't add up, are left as they are.
- **A picture cropped to show different parts of its image in different places is decided for each.** A slides run gave an image one row, covering every copy, so a picture copied and cropped again, an image of icons cropped to a different icon on each slide, took one alt text for all of them. Now each copy of such a picture that needs a decision has a row keyed on its shape (`deck/slide-N/shape-M`), its `Reason` naming the image, which is still written where its key points; copies cropped alike, to within a twentieth of the image on every side, count as one. One alt text on copies that show different parts is a finding (`pptx-alt-same-for-crops`, a warning), and a picture's row in `image-alt.csv` that gives them one is named in a note. A picture with a copy on a layout or master keeps one row, since only its image's key can name that copy.
- **Alt text copied from a web page with its picture is found** (`pptx-alt-page-title`, a warning): the page's title or a stock site's caption, by its form: a stock or clip-art site's name or its words ("stock photo" and its kin at the end or before a dash or a pipe), or, at the end of alt text of 120 characters or fewer, a site's name after a pipe, capitalized and with no bracket in it, or a domain after a dash, or a one-word name after a dash and a title of three words or more, a title cut off with an ellipsis that doesn't follow a comma or a number, a search engine's "What Is It", or a numbered section's title followed by its book's. None of the 45,427 alt texts in 49 of OpenStax's book repositories matches, nor any of the 167 in its statistics decks; without the limit on length, the capital and the brackets after a pipe, the comma before an ellipsis, and "stock photo" placed, it named 12 of OpenStax's alt texts, none a title: a matrix's row (`[0 1 1 | 2]`), a sequence going on (`4, 8, 12, ...`), a long description ending with a quotation's author after a dash, a word processor's Stock Images button, and a figure "isolated on a white background", no longer taken for a stock site's words; in an instructor's decks, every picture it named had a page's title or a stock site's caption for its alt text. A name after a word or two had named labels: 7 of the 2,462 descriptions in a corpus of Word books, Creative Commons' "Attribution - By" and "ShareAlike - SA" among them; with three words, none, and it still names every alt text it named in those decks.
- **Alt text that is only a credit is found**, a warning, in a deck (`pptx-alt-credit`) and wherever a book's alt text is judged (below): who made the picture, its license, or where it's from, and nothing of what it shows, as a caption's credit line or Wikimedia Commons' attribution copied in (`By Jane Roe - Own work, CC BY-SA 4.0, https://…`, `Source: Roe and Doe (2020)`). Each of its sentences opens with a credit's label or is a list of bylines, licenses, "Own work", and addresses, a byline or an address among them; a license's name alone is a license's badge, and isn't one. None of OpenStax's 45,427 alt texts is only a credit, though many end with one; the alt text of two of a corpus of Word books' 2,462 pictures is, one credit on both, and so is that of eight pictures in a set of Markdown decks, six diagrams with one credit line among them.
- **One alt text on different pictures is found**, as a note: in a deck (`pptx-alt-shared`), in a book (a row of `image-alt-missing.csv`, `shared with 1 other image`, and `image-alt-shared` on a page or in an EPUB), and in a source the audit reads (`source-image-alt-shared`), case and closing punctuation aside, where no other rule names it. Pictures are told apart by their images' content, so a picture copied, or one picture in two pages' files, as a Word book's media are, isn't named; the alt text a row of `image-alt.csv` gives is what's compared, so describing one of two pictures anew settles the other. It may be a description copied to a picture it doesn't fit, or one picture in two files of different sizes: 446 alt texts are each on different pictures within one of OpenStax's books, 1,122 pictures in 56 of its 76, and of 40 looked at, about half described different pictures in the same words and the rest were one picture in two files. A PDF's figures aren't compared. `check-output.py --documents` takes a JSON file naming each page's document, within which one alt text is looked for; without it, the pages it's given are one document.
- **Decks written in Markdown are slides too.** In a folder `project.kind` says is slides, each `.md` file is a deck in Pandoc's slide Markdown, read and filtered as a book's page is, with the same sidecars and reports, and written by `html`, `pdf`, `markdown`, and `source` targets (`html` when none is declared); a slides folder holding PowerPoint decks and Markdown files stops, saying to put each kind in a folder of its own. Its slide level is worked out once, as Pandoc works it out, or taken from its own `slide-level`, and the page and the PDF follow it. A deck's page opens with its title, subtitle, authors, and date, its sections and slides headings below, each at its level among the sections around it (an agenda before the first section is an H2, as a section is), its pauses left out, and its speaker notes under each slide in a disclosure, closed by default (`slides.notes`: `collapsed`, `visible`, or `omitted`; `slides.notes_label` for its control's words); `pages.split_level` cuts a deck into a page per section or slide, at a section inside a div too, each piece's headings placed as the page's are. A deck's PDF is built through the ltx-talk class, a presentation class made for LaTeX's tagging, by Pandoc's beamer writer, a template of ours (`bin/ltx-talk.latex`), and LuaLaTeX: claiming PDF/UA-2 (or PDF/UA-1) with its formulas' MathML, its slides' titles tagged as headings at the level of the sections around them and bookmarked under them, a block's title, and a heading in a quotation, a list, or a table, tagged at the level the page gives it, a section shown on a slide of its own headed by its title, a footnote's mark a link to its note and tied to it both ways, as PDF/UA-2 asks, a heading's footnote set after it, in its language, the links of a slide's earlier steps left out, which a screen reader met as links saying "artifact" (presented from the PDF, such a slide's links work on its last step, and the run notes how many were taken out), a code block's spaces kept as characters, a passage in another language tagged with it, links in a color told apart from text, no speaker notes, the shape its author saw (`slides.aspect_ratio`: the deck's `aspectratio`, else 4:3 for a deck written for a beamer theme, else 16:9), what ltx-talk reads differently from beamer put right (code, frame options, columns, footnotes in a table, a caption, and a slide's or a section's title, a numbered list's style, a section's title with an `=`, a link within the deck, beamer's modes), beamer's commands in `header-includes` made harmless and named, and its tables' captions retagged as a book's PDF's are. A LuaLaTeX run that hasn't finished in five minutes is stopped, and a deck whose cross-references still change after four runs is named in a warning. On a course's 25 decks, a conference talk, and a lecture with notes, every PDF passed veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 profile. The run says which slides have no title, which decks name their language `language:`, which Pandoc doesn't read, which have no title, and which have a heading in their speaker notes that sets their slide level. A folder of decks isn't packaged yet. [Slides](docs/slides.md#decks-written-in-markdown).
- **A deck written in Markdown becomes a PowerPoint file** (`format: pptx`), through Pandoc's PowerPoint writer, with what the writer leaves out or gets wrong put right, before it writes (`bin/deck-pptx.lua`) and after (`lib/pptxtarget.py`): each picture's description its alt text, or PowerPoint's decorative mark, where the writer gave every picture its file's path; a figure's own caption shown, as on the page and in the PDF, and one that repeats the alt text marked decorative, as the page hides it from a screen reader, where the writer showed the alt text under every picture; a table's header column flagged, its caption above it and read first, and a table too long for its room, down to the slide's foot or the text the writer puts under it, set smaller, to three quarters of its size at least; tables in PowerPoint's Light Style 1 - Accent 1, where Pandoc's reference document gives Medium Style 2 - Accent 1, whose header row PowerPoint's Accessibility Checker finds too little contrast in; text too much for its box shrunk as PowerPoint's "Shrink text on overflow" would shrink it once edited; a slide cut where the writer would draw a picture over its other content or begin a slide with no title (a second table, a table after a picture, columns with text around them), each part titled with its number, `Supply (2)`; a bulleted list beside a picture given the bullets the master gives a slide's list, where the writer left its paragraphs to that layout's text, whose style gives none; a figure of more than one picture shown as its pictures, its caption under the last, where the writer wrote them as their alt text; an incremental list's animation tied to the shape holding the list; a link to a figure, a table, or a div going to the slide it's on, where the writer took it out; a bar over a letter an accent of the macron, as a Word file's is, which Narrator reads and LibreOffice draws as a bar, where the writer's overline was silent in Narrator and an acute accent in LibreOffice; a display equation, and the text after it, set off by half a line's space, as on the page and in the PDF, where the deck gives no space of its own there, the space above an equation given after the paragraph above it, or, at the top of a slide's text, in the text box's top margin, since PowerPoint shows none before an equation; vertical space the deck gives in raw LaTeX (`\vspace`, `\bigskip`, and the rest), which the writer leaves out, given before the text after it, added to the space it has, in proportion to the deck's PDF; each shape holding an equation given a fallback, its equations as text, which LibreOffice shows, where it had shown nothing of the shape; the deck's language on its text and styles, in place of the reference document's; the file's title, creator, custom properties, and list of slide titles the deck's; unique ids; a link's title its ScreenTip, which the writer doesn't write; each run's properties in the schema's order, where the writer puts a link before a color or a code font; each notes slide's color map, which the writer leaves out before 3.13 and Keynote is reported to need ([Pandoc's issue 11935](https://github.com/jgm/pandoc/issues/11935)): the latest Keynote at the time (15.4 by its release history) opened a test deck with notes as this target wrote it, and not as Pandoc 3.12.1 did; an SVG as a PNG with the SVG beside it; and the XML PowerPoint can't read as written put right, as in a PowerPoint deck's copy. The slides are the deck's shape (`slides.aspect_ratio`) at PowerPoint's size for it, 16:9 its Widescreen, a new deck's size, or with `slides.size: on-screen-show` its older On-screen Show, Pandoc's, on Pandoc's reference document scaled to it, its text too; or `slides.reference_doc`'s, whose default table style is warned of when its header row has too little contrast, and speaker notes are kept unless `slides.notes` is `omitted`, a notes div with another class too and notes before a slide's columns among them, where the writer showed the one on its slide and left the other's heading a slide of its own, a list in them bulleted, as the master bullets a slide's, where the writer left its paragraphs to a notes master that gives none, and a link in them a link, its ScreenTip its title, where the writer takes every link out of the notes (one to a place in the deck stays its words, and the run counts them). What can't be put right is named: pauses, which show nothing, images in a line of text, written as their alt text, passages in other languages, links to a slide with no heading, what the writer leaves out (from its log), shapes it draws in one place, the layouts a reference document lacks, and the writer's own warnings. Each file is checked as a PowerPoint deck is (`check-output.py --pptx`). Needs Pandoc 3.10.1. On a course's 25 decks, a conference talk, a lecture with notes, and 27 small decks, the check finds only what the decks hold. Samples opened in PowerPoint showed as meant, and NVDA and Narrator read them as far as each reads PowerPoint: NVDA announces no table's headers and no animation there, and Narrator reads only a slide's title in Slide Show, as Microsoft documents for PowerPoint for the web. [The PowerPoint file](docs/slides.md#the-powerpoint-file).
- **A deck written in Markdown becomes a reveal.js presentation** (`format: revealjs`), for presenting in a browser, through Pandoc's reveal.js writer and what `lib/revealtarget.py` and the script and stylesheet written into it put right: reveal.js loaded from a copy beside it, the repository's own reveal.js 6.0.2 (`vendor/reveal.js`, unmodified), so nothing needs installing, or the one in the folder `REVEALJS_DIR` names, a release other than the repository's noted, its license and its theme's font's with it, where Pandoc's template loads it from unpkg.com with an address the Nu checker rejects; each picture's address `src`, and loaded with the page, where the writer gives reveal.js's `data-src`, which the Nu checker reports as no address; a viewport a reader can zoom; reveal.js's `role="application"` on the whole presentation taken away and its live region, which announced each slide's text run together, silenced, where NVDA read a slide's formulas as letters and its tables cell by cell, and its progress bar, which NVDA reads as a space, hidden from a screen reader; a slide's title, or a step's content, given focus as it appears, and a link within it, a footnote's mark or its note's way back, focus at its place, where reveal.js did nothing for a place on the slide shown; headings at the levels the deck's page gives them and a block with no title no heading; a section's slides in one row; a slide too tall for its frame fitted, its pictures kept to three fifths of its height and its text set closer, then smaller, in the steps the PowerPoint file's take, where 154 of 339 slides of real decks had run off the window; and a window 1000 pixels wide or less shown the slide as a page, its text the slide's size, which zooming enlarges, where reveal.js scales a slide to its window whatever the zoom, the page's keys scrolling it, as they scroll a slide too tall even fitted. Its slides are the deck's shape, its footnotes at the foot of their slides, its formulas MathML, its look reveal.js's white theme with the PowerPoint file's proportions and links in `#0066CC`, underlined, its arrow back as plain as its arrow on, and its speaker notes in reveal.js's speaker view, shown under each slide, or left out (`slides.notes`). The output check reads a link within a presentation as reveal.js's route. The script that starts reveal.js holds settings and nothing else, or the presentation isn't written and the run stops, naming the setting: a deck's YAML gives the template's settings their values as text, and a value with a quote in it, as a `transition` written in a block of YAML can have, ended its string and put the rest into the presentation as code. Needs Pandoc 3.12. Read with NVDA in Chrome as a page is, its formulas as math and its tables with their headers, a column's as NVDA moves across into it, and in Firefox, as far as it was tried. [The reveal.js presentation](docs/slides.md#the-revealjs-presentation).
- **The output check names a header cell holding only a formula** (`table-header-is-formula` on a page or in an EPUB, `pdf-table-header-is-formula` in a PDF), as a note. NVDA reads such a formula in the header row, but moving into its column it announces no header, in a browser and in Acrobat alike, since it takes a header from the cell's text and a formula has none there; words beside the formula it announces. The detail says which table, and on a page the formula's TeX.
- **A PowerPoint deck is converted as a deck written in Markdown is**, by `html`, `pdf`, `revealjs`, and `markdown` targets, once it's checked and copied: read into the same intermediate by a reader of our own (`lib/pptxsource.py`), since Pandoc's PowerPoint reader keeps no speaker notes, inline formatting, links, equations, numbered or inherited lists, or anything in `mc:AlternateContent` before 3.14, and 3.14's still loses equations and formatting in a table's cells and a slide's title, underline, strikethrough, ScreenTips, the decorative mark, a table's header flags, a notes master's bullets, and the deck's title. It keeps the slides in order, a hidden one left out, PowerPoint's sections, and each slide's title, a slide with none begun with a rule; the title slide as the deck's title and subtitle; text with its runs' own bold, italic, underline, strikethrough, small capitals, and scripts, its line breaks, and its links, to an address (its ScreenTip the link's title) or to a slide; lists as PowerPoint shows them, a paragraph's own bullet or what its shape, layout, master, and presentation give it, nested, in their scheme and from their start; tables with their header row and column and their merged cells; pictures with their alt text, or as decorative, each image written once to `media/` under the key its alt-text row names; equations, through Pandoc's Word reader, which reads the same Office math; and speaker notes. The sidecars' decisions apply as they apply to the copy, and the reports are the decks' own. What it can't keep is named by deck and slide: the rest of a title slide, charts, SmartArt, video and sound, embedded objects, ink, a group's alt text, a picture's crop, a picture in a format a browser doesn't show, and a link that does something else or goes to a slide that isn't shown. A `pptx` target rebuilds each deck on the template `slides.reference_doc` names, and only on one, since a deck's own file is its copy and Pandoc's writer keeps none of its design; the run says what's lost, and without a template writes nothing. `util/round-trip-decks.py` converts a folder's decks to Markdown and back to PowerPoint and compares each with its trip, slide by slide (`lib/deckcompare.py`): on OpenStax's 13 decks nothing differs, in 322 slides, 188 pictures, 95 tables, 73 equations, and the notes of 185 slides, and their 13 PDFs pass veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 profile. [Converting a PowerPoint deck](docs/slides.md#converting-a-powerpoint-deck).
- **`project.kind`**, `book` or `slides`. A folder of decks with nothing a book is read from is slides without it, and says so; a deck in a book's folder is left out of the book, and the run says that too.
- **`images.alt_placeholders`**, the project's own list of alt text that says nothing, for junk a corpus turns out to have (`..`, `slide image`), each matched against the whole alt text with case and closing punctuation aside, or a regular expression after `re:`. Read for a deck's pictures and a book's images alike.
- **A tar of a book's files or a set of decks (`.tar.gz`, `.tgz`, `.tar`, `.tar.bz2`, `.tar.xz`) is unpacked as a zip is**, alone in a folder on the first run: a folder that wraps everything dropped, macOS's leftovers left out, and an entry outside the folder refused. A link or another special file in a tar is refused and listed in `unpack-report.csv`, and so now is a link in a zip, which had been written as a file holding the link's target. A zip's `._` files, which macOS adds beside each file in some archives, are left out too, as `__MACOSX` was.
- **`archive: zip` or `archive: tar.gz`** packs a target's folder into one file beside it, named after it, once it's written. A later run doesn't take the archive for a source to unpack.
- **`audit.py` reads PowerPoint decks**, and `util/remediate.py` writes their copies, from the sidecars named with `--alt`, `--table-headers`, `--slide-titles`, and `--reading-order`.
- **`tests/run-slides-tests.py`**: decks built as PresentationML, each check found in a messy deck and none in a clean one, each decision written into a copy and read back with the rest byte for byte, and `convert.py` on folders of decks, in zips and tars, with no Pandoc on the `PATH` too; and decks read for converting, converted, and taken through the round trip. Breaking each piece of the code in turn, 304 ways, fails it every time.
- **`tests/run-decks-tests.py`**: decks written in Markdown, made up for the purpose, each holding what the code has to handle, written as pages, PDFs, PowerPoint files, and reveal.js presentations and read back. Twelve of its checks open a presentation in a browser with Python's Playwright and its Chromium, which are optional, and skip without them.
- **A book's alt text is judged as a deck's is.** Alt text that holds a file name, says nothing (punctuation alone, a word such as "image", a camera's or tool's name for a file, a shape's default name, a clip-art id, or a pattern in `images.alt_placeholders`), was generated by Office, looks copied from a web page with the picture, or is only a credit is a row of `image-alt-missing.csv` with why, as a missing description is, where only missing and too-long alt text had been; a row in `image-alt.csv` with a blank `Alt` says it was reviewed and stands. The output check finds the same on a page or in an EPUB (`image-alt-placeholder`, `image-alt-auto-generated`, `image-alt-page-title`, `image-alt-credit`, and `image-alt-is-file-name` for a file name anywhere in the alt text) and in a PDF (`pdf-figure-alt-auto-generated`, `pdf-figure-alt-page-title`, `pdf-figure-alt-credit`, and the placeholder and file-name checks it had), and the audit in a Word or Markdown source (`source-image-alt-is-file-name`, `-placeholder`, `-auto-generated`, `-page-title`, and `-credit`), by the built-in rules alone. The rules are one module, `lib/alttext.py`, which a deck's checks use too.
- **`math-keep.csv` keeps a deck's equation as it was, and a slides run reports its math repairs.** Each equation a `source` copy gives the characters it means is a row of `math-repaired.csv`, in a book's columns, its `Page` the deck's file name and its `Before` and `After` the equation written out in one line, much as PowerPoint's linear format writes it (`z=(x–µ)/σ`), since a slides run reads a deck without Pandoc and so has no TeX of it. A row copied from there into `math-keep.csv` keeps that equation as it was, in that deck or, with a blank `Page`, in every deck, and a row that keeps nothing is warned about, as for a book. The run's line about equations says how many were kept.
- **A language babel hasn't installed is named, with how to find its package.** A book in a language, or with a passage in one, whose babel package isn't installed stops LuaLaTeX (babel's `Unknown option 'ngerman'`), as `installation.md` says; the run now names the language, as the book's or a passage's, and the command that names its package, `tlmgr search --global --file /ngerman.ldf` or, on Debian and Ubuntu, `apt-file search /ngerman.ldf`, and says the language's `hyphen-` package has its hyphenation, as it does for a file LaTeX can't find.
- **A report of the ids a page had twice**, `ids-renamed.csv` (`reports.ids_renamed`): for each, the page, the id, the element that kept it, the one renamed, and its new id. A page can't hold an id twice, and the run had only said on the terminal how many ids on a page it renumbered, though a link meant for the one renamed reaches the other: a Word file's bookmark named as Pandoc numbers a repeated heading's id, a Markdown heading with the words of a table's id after it (`# Costs` over `: Costs {#costs}`), where the heading keeps its id since `[Costs]` links to it, or two elements an HTML page gives one id, where the first keeps it, the one a browser follows a link to.
- **`notes.title`**, the heading over an HTML page's notes, `Notes` by default and none when it's empty, and the title of the Notes page `notes.placement: book` makes (Notes when it's empty).
- **A book written in CNXML is a source**, OpenStax's own source format. `unpack-cnx.py` reads an `osbooks-…` repository cloned from GitHub (a bundle's books by `--list` and `--book`) or a book of the Connexions archive, and writes a directory `convert.py` converts: a page per page of the book as OpenStax publishes it, named as its web addresses and Word files name it; its media; `project.yaml` with the book's outline as `contents`, a unit a part; `conversion.yaml` with the footer OpenStax asks every copy to carry, in OpenStax's words, in Spanish or Polish for a book in either, and with its address not a link, footnotes numbered through each chapter, and a book's own word for its captions; `pdf-layout.yaml`, the page its PDF gets, a copy of the PDF target's default (OpenStax's paper and margins with 12-point type, below) to change for the book, on A4 for the two Polish books OpenStax prints on it, and in the face OpenStax sets the book's design in, IBM Plex Serif, IBM Plex Sans, or Noto Sans, larger at a given size than Latin Modern, the default's, the headings in its bold, and a Plex Serif book's formulas in IBM Plex Math, each named by its files, so that a face that isn't installed stops the build; `table-captions.csv` drafted from the tables' summaries, marked as OpenStax's; and a report of what it noticed and didn't decide. The source is baked as OpenStax bakes it, by a Python port of the recipes in openstax/cookbook (MIT License) for all fifty-six styles OpenStax bakes a book in: figures, tables, exercises, and equations numbered, notes titled, each chapter's key terms, summaries, and exercises gathered into pages of their own, solutions moved to an answer key, links given their text, and the index built, in cookbook's English, Polish, or Spanish words, by the book's language, a Spanish book's glossaries and indexes in Spanish order, ñ after n, and its examples' problems headed "Problema", which cookbook has no Spanish for: a book in another language has English ones, and the CNXML page says how its words would be added. Exercises kept in OpenStax's exercise bank are fetched and cached, so a later run, or a machine with no network, needs none (`--offline`); an exercise the cache has isn't fetched again, so a change OpenStax makes to it later is seen only with `--update-exercises`, which asks the bank for each of the book's exercises again and writes a newer version over the cache's copy, each reported; and `util/fetch-exercises.py` fetches them for several repositories' books at once, or from a list of them on a machine without the repositories, and packs the cache to carry to one without the network. It's checked against cookbook's own baking of the 62 sample books it tests with, every page alike; against OpenStax's own assembly of eleven books; and against OpenStax's Word files for six, word for word, *Principles of Marketing* with its 662 exercises from the bank, each by a tool in `util/` (`cnx-compare-cookbook.py`, `cnx-compare-assembly.py`, `cnx-compare-word.py`). What it does differently on purpose, for a book a screen reader reads and no stylesheet of OpenStax's shows, is in the docs: a worked example's steps, a chapter's Key Equations, and a matching exercise are layout tables, OpenStax's labels for tables aren't written, learning objectives numbered by their place are a numbered list, and a note is a box, which every format draws, headed by its kind and its own title in one heading ("Big Picture: A Global Culture?") where OpenStax's baking makes them two, among others (`--no-boxes` leaves the notes unboxed). A character of Microsoft's Symbol font, in Unicode's private use area, is the character it stands for (*Life, Liberty, and the Pursuit of Happiness*'s em dashes), which no font draws as it was. A file outside the repository, named by a path that climbs out of it or by a link in it, isn't copied. [A CNXML book as the source](docs/cnx-input.md).
- **A page's landmarks.** Each page an HTML target writes holds its own content in `<main>`, the `header` setting in a `<header>` before it, and the `footer` setting in a `<footer>` after it, so a screen reader lists the page's banner, main content, and content information, and can move to the content past what every page repeats; a setting that's a `<header>` or a `<footer>` already, as one had to be to make a landmark, is used as it stands. With `menu: on`, the menu goes between the header and `<main>` and the pager between `<main>` and the footer, each a `<nav>`, a navigation landmark of its own. Most pages had no landmark at all, and WAVE said "No page regions"; Pandoc's title block, on a page that has one (the contents page, a deck's page, a page with a subtitle or a date), was a banner apart from the page's content, and is inside `<main>` now with the rest of it. An HTML source's own main element, which Pandoc's reader keeps whole when it has an id or a class (WordPress's `<main id="main" class="site-main">`) or is an element with `role="main"` (Sphinx's), is opened up as a `<section>` is, so the page has one main landmark, its own. The header looks as it did: Pandoc's stylesheet centers a `<header>` over a wide space, meaning its title block, and `page.css` sets that aside for the banner. A page from the pass-through directory is left as it stands. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#header-and-footer).
- **An en dash written for a minus sign in text is made one** (`math.minus_in_text`, on unless turned off): a negative number's sign, where a range's dash can't be (a balance of –$922.0, a slope of –1.5, `(–3, –2)`, 10<sup>–4</sup>), and a dash between two numbers in a run of arithmetic that holds a relation, or that "equal to" or "equals" opens (`1 – 0.05 = 0.95`, `P = 48 – 3Q`), in text and in a picture's description. OpenStax's books write both with an en dash outside any equation, and NVDA, which doesn't say an en dash at its default punctuation level, said nothing for a negative sign's and read a subtraction's as a range, on section 23.1 of Principles of Economics 3e; it reads a minus sign as "minus". A range is left as written, spaced or not (`1990–2000`, `$50,000 – $68,999`), and so are a difference no relation follows and code; an equation's own text is `math.repair_equations`'s (below). Each stretch of text it changes is a row of `math-repaired.csv` of kind `minus`, which a row of `math-keep.csv` keeps as it was, and a Word source's `format: source` copy gets the signs in its runs, their formatting kept, and in its pictures' descriptions, those it can't place counted, as a PowerPoint deck's copy does in its text, its notes, its tables' cells, and its pictures' descriptions, where its page has them (`deckrun.minus_signs`); a deck's copy makes no equation of text, so where its page makes one of the text around a sign, the copy gives that text its sign too. On ten OpenStax books it made 2,166 minus signs, and each of the 277 distinct differences it made written with spaces was a subtraction. [Math](docs/configuration.md#math).
- **An en dash in an equation's own text is a minus sign where the text is math** (`math.repair_equations`), as one typed as text is: a number's sign (`\text{–1}`), a charge's (`\text{Cl}^{\text{–}}`), and a difference, as the economics textbook types its equations as text (`\text{16~–~2P~–~2}`); a text that's the dash alone, with space, is the operator itself. It stays a dash between two letters (a bond, `\text{H–Cl}`), between two digits (a range), beside another dash, and in a text set under or over something as its mark (`\underset{\text{–}}{x}`), as intermediate algebra underlines a blank. An equation's text had kept every en dash, which NVDA, at its default punctuation level, doesn't say. On the 150 distinct formulas of eight OpenStax books whose text holds an en dash, 258 became minus signs and 31 texts that were the dash alone the operator; the 9 left are five chemistry bonds and intermediate algebra's marks. A Word source's `format: source` copy decides its equations' text as Pandoc reads it (runs side by side one text, but each its own in parentheses, a fraction's numerator one again), so each equation reads back as its page has it, as the convert suite checks on fifteen shapes. [Math](docs/configuration.md#math).

### Changed

- **`util/cnx-compare-word.py` reads numbered code with its lines' numbers, and a frame's link to the online book as the Word file's "Access multimedia content"**, as OpenStax's Word files have them, where each counted as a difference: *Introduction to Python Programming*, the one book compared that has either, has 42 pages below 98% alike where it had 84, and every one of the 42 is Word's (2026-11-12).
- **The reference notes are in `notes/`, nine files by subject with an index**, where three were at the top of the repository. [`notes/README.md`](notes/README.md) lists them and says what goes where and how an entry is written. `PANDOC-NOTES.md` and `LATEX-NOTES.md` moved there; `CNX-NOTES.md`, which had been a log of the CNXML work, is a reference on OpenStax's sources and how the unpacker reads them; and six files are new: `OFFICE-NOTES.md`, `WEB-NOTES.md`, `LMS-NOTES.md`, `ACCESSIBILITY-NOTES.md` (the standards, what screen readers said of each output, and what the checkers report), `CORPUS-NOTES.md` (the test books and decks and what converting each measured), and `DESIGN-NOTES.md` (decisions whose reasons aren't obvious from the code). An entry refers to another by file and section and to a measurement by its date, where entries had been numbered, and comments in the code that cited a numbered entry give its date. `CHANGELOG.md` merges by union (`.gitattributes`), so two branches that each add an entry in the same place merge without a conflict.
- **`run-all.sh` runs the test suites side by side, on one count of workers**, where it had run them in turn: `-j` (half the processors by default) is how many things run at once in the whole run. The suites that do one thing at a time run in turn, in order, as one worker; beside them run the convert, EPUB, LaTeX, and decks suites, a case a worker, and the PDF suite, which builds its books as many at once as there are workers free, the longest first, where it had built two at once whatever `-j` said, though it has 23 (`tests/parallel.py`: a lock on one of a folder of files, which the system drops when the process holding it ends). Each suite's output is printed whole, in `run-all.sh`'s order, the PDF suite last; `-j 1` runs every suite in turn, printing as it goes, as before. Ctrl-C stops a run, and a suite whose cases run side by side, which had gone on to start the rest of its cases. And the audit and check suites start epubcheck and the Nu checker only for the checks that read them: the audit suite took 261 s with them and 93 s without, every check passing either way, and the check suite 109 s and 56 s; the audit suite has a case that does read them now. On a ten-core machine a run at `-j 10` took 30 minutes 39 seconds before, with three or four processors at work on average, and 10 minutes 45 seconds after, with nine; at `-j 20`, a worker for each of its twenty threads, it took 10 minutes 21 seconds and over a fifth more processor time, so what bounds a run there now is its work (2026-11-11); at `-j 1`, one thing at a time, it took 50 minutes 26 seconds. With two processors, where little was idle, `run-all.sh -j 2` took 1,639 s before and 1,471 s after. The unit suite checks the workers, and `run-all.sh` itself on stand-ins for its suites. [Testing](docs/testing.md).
- **A deck's table decided `first-column` isn't reported as missing a header row in its copy** (`pptx-table-no-header`), where the copy checks First Column, as one decided `none` isn't: its first column heads its rows, the headers it needs (WCAG 2.0 SC 1.3.1), and no further decision could clear the error, which every run reported, for Table 2.34 in OpenStax's *Introductory Business Statistics 2e* chapter 2 deck once its guess was accepted. PowerPoint's own checker asks for a header row, so it may still list such a table, and the run says so once.
- **The run's note on pages in another language says plainly what each output does with them.** It had said "1 page(s) are in another language than the book's, en-US: ch02 fr (languages.csv). Each target marks each one so, and the book's packages keep the book's language"; it says "1 page is in a different language than the book (en-US): ch02 in fr (languages.csv). Every output marks it in that language; the book as a whole, in its EPUB, its cartridge, and its PDF, stays en-US" now, which is so of a LaTeX book's copy too (above), but for a page of its master's own text, named apart. The book as a whole is said only of what the run makes of it, a cartridge only where the run packages the book, and not of a LaTeX book's PDF from its own files, which is in its copy's language, its preamble's where the project declares none; a slides run's note ends at "Every output marks it in that language", each deck's outputs all being in its language.
- **`convert.py` runs the output check with the Python it's run with**, where it ran `python3`, so a package installed only in a virtual environment, Playwright for `--wcag` on pages, is found when the run is made with that environment's Python ([Installation](docs/installation.md#optional-playwright-and-its-chromium)).
- **A PDF's table columns are counted in ems of the book's own page and faces**, as LuaLaTeX measures them before Pandoc's writer runs (`bin/measure-page.lua`, about seven seconds, in the book's folder, as Pandoc runs LaTeX), where they had been characters, 70 to a line, as for the book class's 10-point type: the default's page, 12-point IBM Plex Serif on 6.5 inches, holds about 84 characters of English, a formula in IBM Plex Math is narrower than its count, and a character of code wider than one of text, so a table whose words fit its line had been set as narrow as it could be, wider than its line, and set smaller to fit, and code in a table's cell set smaller to fit a column narrower than the code. Inline code is counted a character of code each, as a block's lines are. Built whole, *Introductory Business Statistics 2e* has 8 of what's fitted in smaller type, where it had 13, and *Introduction to Python Programming* 60, where it had 69, none smaller than three quarters of its size, where one was at 70 percent. Pictures side by side are measured against the same line, where it had been the book class's, 345 points. The LaTeX opens with the counts as a comment; where LaTeX can't measure the page, the counts are in characters as before, and the run warns why. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A PDF built from a LaTeX book's pages, and its `latex` target, set its PDF and EPS images as the PDF the book's own build includes, and its drawings as the page LaTeX makes of each**, not the SVG its pages show, which made the image's text outlines a reader can't search or copy, a picture in it a resampled copy that blurs when zoomed in, and the image larger: on 121 figures, 6,136 characters of OpenIntro Statistics' 94 lost as text, a photo resampled and stored 7.6 times larger, and OpenIntro's figures a median 1.44 times larger. The reading writes the PDF beside the SVG, `rendered/<path>.pdf`: the image itself, its fonts embedded where it doesn't embed them, as R's `pdf()` device leaves out Helvetica, which PDF/UA requires, or the PDF `epstopdf` makes of an EPS; and a drawing's page, taken from the PDF LaTeX makes of the book's drawings (`pdfseparate`; without it, a drawing is its SVG alone, and the run says so). A glyph a figure's fonts name but map to no text is given the text its name gives it, by the Adobe Glyph List's specification (`vendor/agl/`), which PDF/UA requires: the PDF Ghostscript makes of a matplotlib EPS leaves its minus sign, `minus.case`, mapped to nothing, which veraPDF failed, and passes now. A figure whose own PDF fails PDF/UA otherwise is reported by the output check's veraPDF, as in the PDF a LaTeX book builds from its own LaTeX. The pages, the EPUB, and the Word file show the SVG still. [A LaTeX book as the source](docs/latex.md).
- **An OpenStax book in Noto Sans has its formulas in Noto Sans Math where TeX has its version 3.000**, whose letters are Noto Sans's: `unpack-cnx.py` names it in the book's `pdf-layout.yaml` (`mathfont: NotoSansMath-Regular.ttf`), where it had left the line with a `#` before it, the formulas in Latin Modern Math. Where TeX lacks it, or has only the 2.001 of Debian's and Ubuntu's `fonts-noto-core`, which has no math table, the formulas are set in Latin Modern Math still, and the run warns, saying how to install 3.000, which `luaotfload-tool --update` has to follow. [Installation](docs/installation.md#noto-sans-math).
- **A math font a PDF's metadata file names that TeX lacks no longer stops the build**, which LaTeX had stopped with fontspec's "cannot be found": the formulas are set in Latin Modern Math, and the run warns, naming the font, as it does now for a font with no math table, which unicode-math had passed over for Latin Modern Math saying so in LaTeX's log alone. A font whose options give a `Path`, in the book's own folder, say, is looked for as a file there, since fontspec's test of a font takes no options. The LaTeX a `latex` target writes does the same when it's built. [The faces](docs/configuration.md#the-faces).
- **The decks suite skips its checks of a deck's PowerPoint file and of its reveal.js presentation with a Pandoc older than those targets need**, 3.10.1 and 3.12 (`convert.py`'s `PPTX_PANDOC` and `REVEALJS_PANDOC`), where they failed. Each skip names the Pandoc found and the version needed, and the rest of each case runs: a deck's page, its PDF, and what's said before a target is built. Checked with a stand-in that says it's Pandoc 3.9.0.2 or 3.11 in front of 3.12.1, not with those releases themselves.
- **Pandoc 3.14 is the release to install**, and the installation page installs it; the suites pass with 3.14, 3.13, and 3.12.1 (two processors, 2026-11-12). Its Word writer orders a paragraph's properties another way, which the `docx` target follows (under Fixed), and its LaTeX writer writes a note in a table's cell or a caption as `\pandocfootnotemark` and `\pandocfootnotetext`, which its template defines; a book's PDF comes out the same.
- **Tested with Pandoc 3.13 as well as 3.12.1**, and the installation page installs 3.13. Its PowerPoint writer gives a notes slide the color map the `pptx` target had added, and the target leaves it; its Word writer writes a list item's later paragraphs another way, which the `docx` target follows (under Fixed); and it reads YAML as YAML 1.2, so in a `pdf.metadata` file or a Markdown page's YAML block a setting that's on or off is `true` or `false`, `toc: no` asking for a contents page as any word does, and a key given twice stops the run, where 3.12.1 warned and used the last ([The PDF's page](docs/configuration.md#the-pdfs-page)).
- **A deck's `math-repaired.csv` is in TeX, as a book's is, and has its pages' repairs too.** Its equations were written out in one line, much as PowerPoint's linear format writes them, and when the decks were converted too, what `math.from_text` made of their text, and the pages' equation repairs, weren't reported at all, so a row couldn't be copied from the report to keep one. Each row's `Before` and `After` are Pandoc's TeX now, as its Word reader reads the equation, which is how the page has it, and the pages' rows are there, each under its deck's name; a repair the copy and the page both make is one row, and a row copied into `math-keep.csv` keeps that math in the copy and on the page alike, a row naming an equation in one line still keeping it in the copy. Without Pandoc, with a Pandoc older than 3.9, the release a conversion needs, or where it can't read one, an equation is written out in one line, as before, and the run says so, and why when it's Pandoc; and a run whose conversion stops after the copies are written leaves their rows, and says which rows of `math-keep.csv` kept nothing in the copies. A row that keeps nothing in either is warned about once. A display paragraph of several equations is a display equation for each, on the page as in the copy, so each has a row of its own, which keeps it in both: Pandoc's Word reader reads such a paragraph as one formula, its equations run together. [The reports and the sidecars](docs/slides.md#the-reports-and-the-sidecars).
- **A page's HTML is in its own language**, where every page had the project's (`convert.py`'s `-M lang` overrode a Markdown page's `lang`, an HTML page's, and a CNX module's), and so are a deck's outputs, a deck's PowerPoint file's text among them, which had been the project's too.
- **A copy with no default language of its own gets its page's**, the language most of its text or its runs say, where it had got the project's, so a Word file or a deck whose text is French with no default isn't given English for what names no language; and it's written even when the project declares no language, since it's the file's own. The project's is still written only where the project declares it.
- **A fill-in blank in a tagged PDF is drawn as a line, and is a Span whose ActualText is "blank".** A screen reader was left to read a blank's underscores as its punctuation setting has it; it says "blank" in their place now, as NVDA said it for each blank of a page of *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s exercises, and copying gives the word where the viewer takes ActualText (not tried here). A blank is a run of three or more underscores in the text, or of two that stands apart, not joined to a letter, a digit, a character outside ASCII, or anything that isn't text, as emphasis or a formula, a run of four or more being drawn shorter to fit its line where it must: runs of three and two count now, since cells of them side by side, as *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s contingency tables have them, ran together in the text as runs of six, while Python's `__init__` keeps its underscores. The line is where the underscores' ink was, end to end and as thick, unbroken where IBM Plex Serif leaves a gap between two underscores, and the PDF's text has no character for it: veraPDF's WCAG check for repeated characters (1.3.1-1), which reads the characters, not ActualText, reported each blank of five or more underscores, as PDF4WCAG did in *Introductory Business Statistics 2e*'s exercises, and with the underscores gone has nothing of a blank to read: PDF4WCAG's machine checks of the book's PDF built so found nothing but its reference lists' bare links (2.4.9-1). Copying from a viewer that doesn't take ActualText gives nothing for a blank now, where it gave the underscores, as poppler's `pdftotext`, which Evince and Okular are built on, Ghostscript, pdfminer.six, and pypdf each copied it (measured). Blanks with nothing but spaces between them, as an answer of two words has, are one Span, "blank blank", whose spaces have no character in the text, which would otherwise be three side by side for two blanks, which veraPDF's check 1.3.1-2 reports; the spaces can be any of Unicode's, a thin space among them, and a blank in bold or italics can be one of the blanks. The line's measure is the face's underscore's, an OpenType or TrueType face's, whether luaotfload or HarfBuzz shapes it (`Renderer=HarfBuzz`); a blank in a face loaded from TeX's own metrics, which have none, keeps its underscores, and the build warns, naming the face. An untagged PDF keeps them too, with no ActualText to give the blank's text. Underscores that aren't a blank (a heading's, a link's, code's, and a run lua-ul draws a line under or through) are drawn as before. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A table's notes on the rows above them are no bands, and no column heads its rows.** A row one cell across that ends as a sentence does, after a row of the table's cells, in a table that ends with one, is a note, not a band over the rows below it: the split doesn't cut the table there, and a table with such a note before its last row is guessed to have no header column, since no cell of a note's row could head it, and a writer cuts a cell across a body's row-head columns. A guess of `both` is `first-row`; one of `first-column` is `first-row` where the source marks a header row (a `<thead>`, a Word table's first row its table look formats, a row marked to repeat) and `none` where nothing does; and `unknown` is `none` where nothing does, but for a table whose header row is below a data row. *Prealgebra 2e*'s Table 3.1, two cases side by side in pairs of rows, each pair closed by such a note, had been guessed `first-column` and split at its first note, the note the second part's caption; it's `none`, whole, and so is its Table 3.4, which no rule recognized. Of the 15,935 tables the census's two corpora hold, six are guessed otherwise: those two, their copies in the chapter's Key Concepts and a table of *Intermediate Algebra 2e*'s, all three layout tables, and *Clinical Nursing Skills*' steps in two columns with a step across both first and last, `first-row` still but no longer split at its first step. A sentence over the rows of a table that ends with them, as a self-test's statement is over its scale, is a band still, and so is one under another row one cell across; a table that opens with such a sentence is one of bands, as a self-test closed by its total's line is; and a line at a table's foot that says where it comes from ("Adapted from …") is no note. [Sidecars](docs/sidecars.md).
- **What a book in another language needs is on the installation page**: TeX's support for the language (babel's file for it, without which LuaLaTeX stops, and its hyphenation patterns, without which babel hyphenates it as another language), and how to install Spanish's, with `tlmgr` or with `apt`. [Installation](docs/installation.md#what-each-is-for).
- **The `footer` setting is on every page of every format**, where only an HTML target's pages had it: the end of each of an EPUB's content documents but its cover and its navigation document, after the notes a chapter's file ends with; the foot of every page of a PDF, and of the PDF a `latex` target's master builds, the title page and each chapter's first among them, centered in small type under whatever the page's style puts there, an artifact as a page number is; every page of a Word file, in a footer part of its own; and the end of every Markdown or AsciiDoc file's text, in a div of class `oer-footer` (in AsciiDoc, an open block with that role) written as the rest of the file is, which reading the file back as a source takes out again, since the target's own setting puts it back. Outside an HTML page a picture in it is its alternative text and a link to one of the book's own files its words, since a target carries neither file, a heading in it is a paragraph, kept out of the contents and the bookmarks, and an id in it is left out, since the footer is on every page; in a PDF and a Word file any link is its words, since a link in a PDF's foot would be a link outside the document's structure. So a footer set in `defaults` reaches every target now; one meant for the pages alone goes on their target. A deck's PDF, PowerPoint file, and reveal.js presentation get none, nor does a LaTeX book's PDF from its own build (`pdf.from: book`), or a `source` target's copy. OpenStax asks every copy of one of its books, digital or printed, to carry its attribution on every page, which `unpack-cnx.py` writes as the footer, and the EPUB and the PDF hadn't had it. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#header-and-footer).
- **A Word source's accent of the macron (U+00AF) is read as `\bar`**, where Pandoc's reader takes it for `\overline`: it's the accent LibreOffice writes for its bar, and the one the math repair and the `docx` target now write, so their files read back as `\bar`, as the pages had it. Pandoc alone still reads `\overline`.
- **A PDF/UA-1 file's formulas carry Microsoft Office's MathML attribute by default** (`pdf.ua1_math: office`, which had been an option), beside the TeX alternative text and the attached MathML LaTeX gives each. NVDA reads the attribute before anything else, and in Acrobat it read such a file's formulas as math, where with the old default it read their TeX source aloud, "LaTeX formula starts" and all. `pdf.ua1_math: alt` leaves the attribute out, as before, and the run's warning about a PDF/UA-1 book's equations says which it did.
- **A folder of `.pptx` files alone is converted as slides**, where the run had stopped with nothing to convert, and a deck beside a book's pages is named as left out, where it had been passed over without a word. The convert suite's check that a deck isn't unpacked as an archive says so.
- **The output check finds a link whose label leaves out its text in an EPUB too** (`link-label-without-text`), as it did on a page; an EPUB's content documents weren't checked for it.
- **Two notes in a row are marked "1,2"**, with a comma between their marks, set as the marks are, in every format but a Markdown or AsciiDoc copy, which keeps what the author wrote: their marks had read as one number, "12". That's two notes written together and several in one link's text, which go after the link together; LaTeX's footmisc package sets them so with its `multiple` option. Two notes a space parts get none. Two in a heading or a caption get one too, set where their words are copied without the notes, a contents entry, a bookmark, a running head, a list of figures' entry, a page's title, or an EPUB's navigation, so that no comma stays there: in the pages and the EPUB, drawn by `page.css` in an empty span of class `note-comma` right after a note's mark; in the PDF and the `latex` target, a comma the writer leaves out of a heading's contents entry and bookmark, and the filter out of a caption's list entry. A Word file's heading and caption get none, its contents copying their words. A term's notes get one, in the PDF at the head of its definition, where they're set. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **An empty header cell is a data cell**, on a page, in an EPUB's chapters, and in a presentation (`lib/headercells.py`). Pandoc's HTML writer makes every cell of a head row a `<th>`, and every cell of a header column, whatever it holds, so a table whose rows are labeled down its first column had an empty `<th>` at its top left, a header that heads nothing: axe-core reports one (`empty-table-header`, among its best practices), and Chrome exposed it as a column header with no name, where now it's a cell. W3C's tables tutorial codes such a corner as an empty `<td>`. Its attributes are kept but `scope` and `abbr`, and on a page and in an EPUB it's marked (`class="empty-header"`) for `page.css` to draw the line and the room Pandoc's stylesheets give a header cell, so the table looks as it did. Principles of Economics 3e's pages had 36 such cells on 23 pages, 3 of them in a header column, and Introductory Business Statistics 2e's 47; checked with axe-core's best practices, the economics book's has none left. The copy of an HTML source keeps an empty cell of a header row or column the table-headers sidecar declares a `<td>`, where it had made it a header cell.
- **A quotation is a caption's gray, which clears WCAG's AAA** (`page.css`): #555, 7.33:1 on a page's #fdfdfd, and #b6b6b6 in dark mode, 8.58:1 on a page's #1a1a1a and 7.50:1 on an EPUB's #232629. Pandoc's stylesheet gives a page's quotation #606060, 6.18:1, which passes AA and not AAA, as axe-core found on Principles of Economics 3e's 12 quotations (`color-contrast-enhanced`, 15 elements on 11 pages), and finds on none of them now; its EPUB stylesheet gives one no color, so an EPUB's quotations, which were the text's color, are gray too. A printed quotation is black, as a caption is: Pandoc's print rule for one comes before the color it gives one, which took its place.
- **The PDF suite says which of fontTools and LuaLaTeX its checks of a CFF2 variable font lack**, and how to install fontTools, where its skip named both ("no fontTools or no LuaLaTeX"): fontTools is for the tests alone, which make the font with it. The check of a book set in the font is two, built in both of luaotfload's modes and warning once, and each says, when it fails, what the book's run built and said, which the suite doesn't keep.

### Deprecated

- **`build-cartridge.py --includeallhtml` is `--include-all-html`**, hyphenated as every other option of more than one word is, and `convert.py` passes it on as before. The old name still works, with a warning that names the new one, and goes after 1.0.

### Fixed

- **With Pandoc 3.14, a table in an aligned table's cell kept the wrong alignment in a Word file** (`lib/docxtarget.py`). Pandoc writes such a cell's paragraphs with two alignments, the cell's own and the cell's around it, where Word's schema allows one, and the Word target keeps the cell's own. Through 3.13 that's the first of the two. 3.14 writes them in the order of their names, `center` before `right` whichever is which, so a right-aligned cell in a centered cell came out centered. With 3.14 the cell's own is now worked out from the table: it's the alignment all the cell's paragraphs share that the cell around it doesn't have, and where that leaves two, the other cells of the column say. A column of one cell holding nothing but a table aligned one way throughout says nothing, and there the first is kept, as before. The convert suite's check, which 3.14 failed, passes with 3.13 and 3.14, and the unit suite has both forms, so a run with either Pandoc checks both. The convert suite's check of a caption's two notes in the `latex` target takes 3.14's `\pandocfootnotemark{n}` as it takes `\footnotemark{}`: 3.14 writes a deferred note's mark and text with macros of its template's ([#11936](https://github.com/jgm/pandoc/issues/11936)), and a book's PDF and its `latex` target's files are the same in what they show with either (measured on a page with 18 notes, 2026-11-12; [`notes/PANDOC-NOTES.md`](notes/PANDOC-NOTES.md#the-latex-writer-for-a-tagged-pdf)).
- **A biblatex book's PDF from its pages wasn't built** (`bin/pdf-target.lua`). Pandoc's LaTeX writer makes an entry of the bibliography Pandoc's citeproc makes an item of its list, and a citation's link a link to it, only where the entry's id begins `ref-`, and the book's assembly gives every id its page's prefix first, so the entries were paragraphs in the list, and LaTeX stopped ("Something's wrong--perhaps a missing \item"). The `latex` target wrote the same LaTeX. An entry's id begins `ref-` again, the page's prefix after it, and each citation links to its entry, described by its words, and is titled by them in a list of figures' entry. [LaTeX books](docs/latex.md).
- **In a PDF, words beside a column of fill-in blanks too wide for them to share its line with the blanks at their least no longer wrap onto more lines than they need**: the column of blanks is held at its least, as a column of words is at its longest word, where it had taken its share of the line by its blanks' whole length (a table of 23 words beside a label and a blank of 66 underscores: four lines, now three) (2026-11-12).
- **In a PDF/UA-1 book, a link LaTeX makes on its own could be described with an en dash for two hyphens, or cut off at a backslash** (`lib/latexbuild.py`). Its description was made with hyperref's `\pdfstringdef`, which takes `--` for a dash, and a replacement kept a hyphen from the next only outside a brace group and only in an address, so `\url{https://example.org/{a--b}/c}` and `\hyperlink{pre--and-post}` were described with "a–b" and "pre–and-post", and an address with a backslash in it ended there. It's made as hyperref's own description is now, which keeps what's written. Only a book's own LaTeX makes such a link. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A formula opening with `\displaystyle` that texmath can't read is warned of once**, by the page's writer, where `target-blocks.lua`, writing it in display style first, warned of it too (2026-11-12).
- **Groups nested more than three levels deep are one warning**, which counts them and names them, where each was a warning of its own: OpenStax's *World History, Volume 1* holds a group of assessments under a section of each of its 17 chapters, and a run of its pages and EPUB said so 51 times, three for each group (2026-11-11). It says so three times now, each time once.
- **An anchor or a passage in another language in a caption put LaTeX's words in the title of the caption's entry in a PDF's list of figures or tables** (`lib/latexbuild.py`). LaTeX titles the entry's structure element with its text made plain, which kept "[section]" from hyperref's `\phantomsection`, Pandoc's anchor for a span's id, at the title's start, and the language's name from babel's `\foreignlanguage`, "frenchmot" for a French word. The title is the caption's words now. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A picture in a figure's caption stopped a PDF with a list of figures** (`lib/latexbuild.py`). The writer sets a picture without a size in `\pandocbounded`, which its template defines as a fragile command, and doesn't protect it outside a heading, so the caption went to the list's file with the command expanded, and LaTeX stopped reading it ("Missing control sequence inserted"). The command is robust now, and the entry's title in the tagged PDF has the picture's alt text in its place: "A figure whose caption has a red square in it." [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **Five statements in the documentation that the notes or a measurement contradicted are corrected.** `docs/cnx-input.md` said OpenStax's own Word files have the answers the exercise bank doesn't make public; the one comparison made (2026-11-04, *Foundations of Information Systems*) found its Word answer key holding only the public ones. `docs/formats.md` said whether Acrobat's copy keeps a code block's lines apart hadn't been tried; it does, without their indentation (Rob, 2026-11-11). `docs/auditing.md` said Ace stays by hand, where it runs on each EPUB with `--wcag`. `docs/latex.md` dated graphicx 1.2e 2024-12-31, where TeX Live 2026's file is dated 2026-05-17. And `docs/latex.md`, the run's own line, and `resolve_graphic` said LaTeX on Linux doesn't find an image named in another case than its file's: TeX Live 2026's LuaLaTeX does, kpathsea's `texmf_casefold_search` being on, and doesn't with it off (measured 2026-11-11), so the line now says it may not.
- **A block of code copied from Acrobat was one line** (`lib/latexbuild.py`, `lib/texremediate.py`). LaTeX's tagging tags each line of a block of code as a Sub, PDF 2.0's element for a line of source code, but its role map for a reader that knows only PDF 1.7's tags made each line a Span, so to such a reader the block was one run of text, and a block of two lines copied from Acrobat was one line. In that role map a line is a P now, in a book's PDF, a deck's, and a LaTeX book's tagged copy where the book has a block of code; a reader of PDF 2.0 reads a Sub, as before. Whether Acrobat's copy now keeps the lines apart hasn't been tried. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A PDF built with LaTeX 2026-11-01 is tagged as one built with 2026-06-01 is** (`lib/latexbuild.py`, `lib/texremediate.py`). LaTeX 2026-11-01, in TeX Live 2026 since November 2026, sets headings and lists with new code, gives a paragraph's structure and a heading's new names, and its emulation of enumitem has keys the earlier one lacked, and the run's fixes to LaTeX's tagging reached into what it replaced. With it, a LaTeX book's copy using enumitem's settings stopped ("The key 'template/block/std/left-margin' is unknown"), and `resume=name` set a list's item with no number; a list's item opening with highlighted code lost its number; a heading's number and title had two spaces between them in the text a screen reader gets, and a chapter's number and title none ("Chapter 1Alphaone"); and a tagged copy built to DVI stopped on `\pdffakespace`, which its headings call ("not allowed in DVI mode"). Each fix reads what the release in use has: LaTeX 2026-11-01's emulation of enumitem sets `leftmargin=*`, `labelindent`, and `ref` itself, which the copy leaves to it, and the run's message says which settings each release takes.
- **A heading's contents entry and bookmark went to its last note**: they and a `\label` after the heading went to the note's text at the foot of the page, in a book's tagged PDF and a LaTeX book's copy, with LaTeX 2026-06-01 and 2026-11-01 alike. LaTeX's footnote code, which `\DocumentMetadata` loads, makes a note's text a link target and leaves it the current one after the note; with 2026-11-01 the contents entry's structure referred to the note, which veraPDF's PDF/UA-2 check fails, the note not referring back. Each note's text puts the target back as it ends.
- **A deck's PDF tagged every slide's title at one level with ltx-talk 0.6.11** (`bin/ltx-talk.latex`). ltx-talk 0.6.11 (November 2026) sets a slide's title in a box the text's width, so its code for the title wasn't code the template knew, and the PDF fell back to tagging every slide's title at the level below the deck's deepest sections, as the run warned: a slide before a deck's first section was an H2 beside its sections' H1s, so its PDF's first heading wasn't an H1, as PDF/UA-1 wants, and veraPDF failed it (7.4.2), and a slide in a section without subsections was an H3 in a deck that has them. The template knows 0.6.11's code too, and tags each slide's title at the level of the sections around it again, as with 0.6.9 and 0.6.10.
- **A LaTeX book in memoir couldn't be built tagged with a `\title`, an `\author`, or a footnote** (`lib/texremediate.py`). memoir 3.8.4b changes `\title` and `\author` as it loads, and under tagging they're LaTeX's tagging's own, which its changes don't fit, so a `\title` or an `\author`, the book's or the one the copy gives a book that names none, stopped LaTeX ("Extra \endgroup"); and with hyperref loaded, as the copy loads it for the bookmarks, memoir's changes for hyperref (memhfixc) step a counter for each footnote's mark that hyperref doesn't make where `\DocumentMetadata` is given, so the first footnote or `\thanks` stopped it ("No counter 'Hfootnote' defined"), with LaTeX 2026-06-01 and 2026-11-01 alike. The copy gives memoir LaTeX's own `\title` and `\author` to change, as it does untagged, and keeps the footnote mark memhfixc would replace, and the run says so: a memoir book's PDF builds, with its title and author, a note's mark a link to its note and a `\pagenote`'s to its endnote, and veraPDF passes a small one with no contents; memoir's own contents still fail it, as before ([LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#a-classs-own-contents)).
- **Two footnote marks in succession ran together in a memoir book's tagged PDF**, "12" for "1,2" (`lib/texremediate.py`). LaTeX's footnote code, which `\DocumentMetadata` loads, sets a note's mark with code of its own in memoir's place, which has none of memoir's comma between marks in succession (`\multfootsep`), with LaTeX 2026-06-01 and 2026-11-01 alike. The copy gives memoir's own two macros for it to that mark's hooks, and the run says so. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#the-source-target).
- **A LaTeX book in amsart, amsproc, amsbook, or memoir with no `\maketitle` had no title in its tagged PDF** (`lib/texremediate.py`), which PDF/UA-2 requires, and veraPDF failed it: those classes define `\title` and `\author` of their own, which replace LaTeX's tagging's, and LaTeX's tagging takes the title and author from them only at `\maketitle`, so a book that sets its title page itself, or names no title and gets the copy's `\title`, had none. The copy takes them from `\title` and `\author` at the end of the preamble, where LaTeX's tagging has none yet and the book names no `pdftitle`, and the run says so. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#the-source-target).
- **A table's footnote marks were counted wider than LaTeX sets them in a book whose chapters are numbered** (`bin/pdf-target.lua`, `bin/measure-page.lua`). The book class numbers notes from 1 again at each chapter it numbers, but the PDF's table counts gave every mark as many digits as the book's last note has, so where the book's notes ran to more digits than a table's own, a column held at a word with a mark after it was a digit's advance wider than it needed, 0.39 of an em in IBM Plex Serif at 12 points, and its table could be set smaller to fit. The build measures whether the book's class numbers its notes by chapter, and counts a table's marks as wide as its last note's number as LaTeX gives it: from 1 again at each numbered chapter of the main matter and the appendices, on through the front and back matter and an unnumbered chapter.
- **A figure's caption with a note in a deck's PowerPoint file was hidden from a screen reader, the note's mark with it**: a caption whose words repeat its picture's alt text is marked decorative, as the page hides one, and the writer writes a note's mark in the caption, a number linked to the notes' slide, which PowerPoint's mark, on the whole text box, hid too. A caption with a note isn't marked now, and is read whole, after the alt text, its words twice; a figure of two pictures' too, and a picture's own caption with a note in such a figure is kept before the figure's, where it gave way to it and its note was lost. [The PowerPoint file](docs/slides.md#the-powerpoint-file).
- **A figure's caption with a link of the author's in a deck's PowerPoint file was hidden from a screen reader, the link with it** (`bin/deck-pptx.lua`): a caption whose words repeat its picture's alt text is marked decorative, as the page hides one, and PowerPoint's mark, on the whole text box, hid the link too, as it had a note's mark. A caption with a link isn't marked now, and is read whole, after the alt text, its words twice, as the page shows one; a figure of two pictures' too, and a picture's own caption with a link in such a figure is kept before the figure's, where it gave way to it and its link was lost. [The PowerPoint file](docs/slides.md#the-powerpoint-file).
- **A LaTeX book's tagged copy stopped LaTeX, or took the project's language for the book's, where the book's own `\DocumentMetadata` had comments** (`lib/texremediate.py`). The copy gives a book's own `\DocumentMetadata` the keys tagging needs that it lacks (`tagging=on`, a `lang`, the standard it claims), and wrote its keys again on one line, comments and all: a comment after its last key took the keys added and the closing brace, so LuaLaTeX stopped ("File ended while scanning use of \DocumentMetadata"), and a comma in a comment hid the key after it, so a book's own `lang=de-DE` was given a second `lang`, the project's `en`, which LaTeX took for the PDF's. Its keys are read without its comments now, its lines and comments kept, `tagging` made `on` where it stands, and the keys added put after the last, before a comment on its line. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#the-source-target).
- **The run's line for a LaTeX book's other masters didn't say they're given babel's `main=`** (`bin/convert.py`). A tagged copy gives each master it writes babel's `main=` where the book names no main language of its own, so that babel keeps the book's language rather than taking `\DocumentMetadata`'s `lang` for it, and the run said so of the book's master alone; its line for the other masters now names it too, or says where it's left to the `lang`.
- **A LaTeX book's tight definition list lost its terms** (`lib/latexsource.py`). Pandoc's LaTeX writer writes `\tightlist` before a tight list's first `\item`, and its reader, which skips anything there in an `itemize` or `enumerate`, doesn't in a `description`, so it read the list as a block of its definitions: every term was lost, in a `latex` target's copy read back as a source too, with Pandoc 3.12.1, 3.13, and 3.14. Code before a description's first `\item` is moved before it in the reading's copy now. [What is put right before reading](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book written by Pandoc 3.14 lost its deferred notes when read with an older Pandoc** (`lib/latexsource.py`). 3.14's LaTeX writer defers a note out of a table's cell, a caption, or a term as `\pandocfootnotemark{n}` and `\pandocfootnotetext{n}{...}`; read with 3.13 or older, each such note was lost and its macro's name ("pandoc@fn@1") left in the text. Each is a `\footnote` where its mark is now, which every Pandoc the run takes reads. [What is put right before reading](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book's quotation holding a paragraph break was a quotation in each paragraph** (`lib/latexsource.py`). csquotes' `\enquote` sets one quotation over its paragraphs, an opening mark in the first and a closing mark in the last, and the pages had a quotation in each, both marks in both: "A « un »" and "« deux » fin." where LaTeX sets "A « un" and "deux » fin.". Its marks are written in as LaTeX sets them now, in Pandoc's marks too, a quotation inside it with its own, and so are those of a quotation holding a list, which LaTeX also sets as one, and the run counts each quotation it gives other marks once, where it counted each paragraph's. [What is put right before reading](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book that selects its language at `\begin{document}` had its pages in babel's main language** (`lib/latexsource.py`). `\AtBeginDocument{\selectlanguage{english}}`, or `\AddToHook{begindocument}` with one, sets the text in that language wherever the preamble sets the hook, before babel is loaded too (measured with LuaLaTeX, babel 26.13), and the reading took babel's main language, French with `[english,french]`, for the pages' `lang`, which a screen reader picks its voice by, the project sample's, and a tagged copy's; and under csquotes' `autostyle=true` it gave the quotations French's marks, where LaTeX sets English's. The last language such a hook selects is the book's now, and autostyle's outside a passage; the default `tryonce` keeps the main language's marks, as csquotes does. [How a book is read](docs/latex.md#how-a-book-is-read).
- **A LaTeX book's PDF from its own files was counted as tagging a page its master's `\includeonly` leaves out** (`lib/texremediate.py`, `bin/convert.py`). LaTeX's build reads only the files `\includeonly` names, and Pandoc's reader reads every `\include`, so the PDF lacked pages the other targets had, and the run, saying nothing of that, counted such a page in another language as tagged in it ("1 page(s) tagged in their own language (ch2 fr)") and gave a copy a line for it. A page left out isn't counted or given a line now, in the PDF or a `source` target's copy, and the run warns that the PDF leaves it out, naming it. [The PDF target](docs/latex.md#the-pdf-target).
- **A LaTeX book's file with a dot in its name took the place of the master's own page** (`lib/latexsource.py`, `bin/convert.py`). `\include` takes a `.tex` off a name and reads the name with `.tex` added, so `\include{week1.5}` reads `week1.5.tex`, and the reading named its page as if `.5` were an extension: `week1`, the name of the master `week1.tex`'s own page, which it was written over, the master's own words lost; the contents sample listed `week1` twice, and the file's row in `languages.csv` matched no page, so a copy didn't tag it in its language. Its page is `week1.5` now. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#how-a-book-is-read).
- **A LaTeX book's passage named by another of babel's names for its language, or by its name in capitals, had no language in its pages** (`lib/latexsource.py`). babel sets `\foreignlanguage{francais}{…}` and `\foreignlanguage{French}{…}` in French, taking language.dat's synonyms and a name in capitals for the language (measured with LuaLaTeX, TeX Live 2026), and Pandoc's reader knows neither name, so the passage had no `lang`, in `\foreignlanguage` and `otherlanguage` alike. The reading writes the name the reader knows (`french`, `american` for `USenglish`), some without their region (`canadien` as `french`), and the run counts them. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A formula in a slide's title was its characters run together in a deck's PowerPoint file's list of slide titles** (`lib/pptxtarget.py`), "The x2 law" for `# The $x^2$ law`, a root what's under it ("Why x grows") and a fraction its two parts ("A ab ratio"), as the file's parse reads a formula's text. That list, in the file's properties, is what a program that lists a deck's slides by their titles may show, though PowerPoint writes it afresh when it saves the deck. A formula there is its text now ("The x² law", "Why √x grows", "A a/b ratio"), as in the formula's fallback. [The PowerPoint file](docs/slides.md#the-powerpoint-file).
- **A Word display paragraph of more than one equation was one formula in a book's pages, its equations run together** (`lib/docxrepair.py`). PowerPoint shows each equation of a display paragraph (`m:oMathPara`) on a line of its own (Rob, 2026-11-04; not tried in Word), and Pandoc's reader reads the paragraph whole as one formula, `{a = 1}{b = 2}` for two (measured on 3.12.1 and 3.13), so a page, an EPUB, and a PDF showed them as one. The reading's copy of a Word file makes each such equation a display paragraph of its own, in the body, the footnotes, and the endnotes, so each is a display formula, as a deck's reader already reads them; a paragraph holding anything else between its equations is read as before. How often Word books hold one wasn't measured. [Formats](docs/formats.md#word).
