- **An untagged PDF of a book with a table with a header column, or a table in a table's cell with a header row, wasn't built.** With `pdf.standard` naming no PDF/UA standard, LaTeX loads no tagging, and the `\tagpdfsetup` that declares such a table's headers stopped it ("Undefined control sequence"). It's written only where it's defined now, as a cell spanning rows already had it.
- **A page's notes had no heading**, so a screen reader had none to move to them by, and the Nu checker warned on every page with notes that their section lacks one. They're headed `Notes` now, after the rule above them, on every HTML page (`notes.title` for the book's language, or none), and on the `notes` page `notes.placement: book` makes, each group's notes are headed by its group's title inside their section, in the pages and the EPUB alike; that page is titled with `notes.title` too, where its `<title>` had been `notes`. An EPUB's notes at the end of a chapter have no heading, since a reading system may show each note beside its reference.
- **A PDF with a matrix or an array in a table's cell or caption wasn't built.** LaTeX's tagging reads a formula whole before setting it, and TeX took the formula's `&` for the end of the table's cell ("Forbidden control sequence found while scanning use of `\__math_grab_inline_delim:w`"), or, in a caption, for the table's while nameref read its title. OpenStax's dev-math books check a solution that way in their worked examples' steps, an array of its equations inline in a table's cell, on 64 pages of the three, and LaTeX stopped on the first (measured on *Elementary Algebra 2e*'s section 5.2). Such a formula is set in braces now, and is a Formula with its MathML in its cell or caption.
- **A PDF's caption that carries its own label was numbered twice.** LaTeX gives every caption a label of its own, so a book whose captions number themselves, as OpenStax's do, had "Figure 1: Figure 1.1 The real number line" and "Table 1: Table 1.1 Sets of numbers", and in *College Algebra*'s first chapter "Figure 2: Figure 1 The real number line", LaTeX counting every figure; a screen reader read both, LaTeX's tagged as the caption's label. A caption that opens with a word `captions.figure_prefixes` or `captions.table_prefixes` names and then a number gets no label of LaTeX's now, tagged or not, its Caption holding its words and no empty label element; one that doesn't keeps LaTeX's. And a figure the book marks unnumbered (class `unnumbered`, on the figure or, in Markdown, its image), as OpenStax marks a chapter's opening picture, gets none either, as an unnumbered table had none: *College Algebra*'s read "Figure 1: Credit: …". A deck's PDF, where ltx-talk's label is "Figure:" or "Table:" with no number, does the same, a table's caption too, and takes the words from the deck's `captions.*` settings, as a book's does.
- **A table or a div lost its id to an HTML page's heading with the same words.** Pandoc's reader makes a heading's id from its words when the page gives it none, checking only the headings before it, so `<h1>Money</h1>` over `<table id="money">` gave both the id `money`. Of two elements with one id the first keeps it, so the table became `money-1`, and the author's link to it reached the heading, a valid link that no check could fault. Nothing on a page can link to an id its author never wrote, so such a heading gives way now, wherever it stands: before the table or div, or inside a div with the id (`htmlrepair.py` marks it before Pandoc reads the page). One the page gives an id of its own, through a section around it or an anchor in it, keeps it, and the other's rename is a row of `ids-renamed.csv`. And a renamed element's new id is one nothing on the page has, where it could be the id of an element further down, which then lost its own in turn.
- **A page grouping a table by `<tbody>`s that each open with a row of `<th>`, a group's own header row, split it into parts with no header row**, and a table split at its bands, each over a group's header row, lost those rows too when read back from a Markdown or AsciiDoc target. The page's `<th>` cells declared a header column alone, which moved each part's header row into its body; it heads its part now, as a row of several cells named in `split-at` does. And a group's header row under its band, in the copy a `<tbody>`'s head below the band, had come back as a row of data, so the parts had no header row and a copy of the copy no groups; it heads its part again, as the guess made it.
- **`notes.numbering: group` and `notes.placement` grouped a book's notes one way in its HTML pages and another in its EPUB.** The pages took a page's group to be what the split made of its source: in a book of a file per page, such as an OpenStax book from its Word files or a Markdown book whose `contents` holds its chapters, every page was a group of its own, so `group` changed nothing and the Notes page listed the pages in the order of their files' names; a single-file book cut at its H1s was one group, cut at its H2s its chapters' own pages were apart from their sections, under the source's title on the Notes page, and chapter files cut at their H3s numbered a section with subsections apart from the rest of its chapter. The EPUB took the top-level entry of the contents, and so numbered across a whole part. Both take a page's group from the book's contents now, declared or guessed: the top-level entry holding it, a chapter with all its pages or a page on its own, or in a group of `type: part` the part's entry holding it, so a part's chapters are numbered each on its own; the groups go in the contents' order, and the Notes page's headings are numbered when the book is. [Footnotes across pages](docs/splitting.md#footnotes-across-pages).
- **A note's link to an id on its own page went nowhere on the page `notes.placement` moved the note to.** A link within the page (`#methods`) was left as it was, so on a group's last page or the Notes page it named an id that page hasn't, which the output check reported; an EPUB's links between its chapter files already found the id. It names the note's page now (`ch1--methods.html#methods`).
- **A note holding a numbered list lost its end where `notes.numbering` or `notes.placement` rearranged the notes.** A note was taken to the first `</li>` before an `</ol>`, which a numbered list inside it has, so it was cut off after the list's last item, its way back lost and the next note left inside its list. A note now runs to the next note's item, or to the end of the notes.
- **A one-page book's EPUB took no notice of `notes.numbering: group`, and `notes.placement: book` left its Notes chapter empty.** The writer cuts such a book into a chapter file per top-level heading, and the notes' arrangement found none of them among the book's groups, so each file's notes stayed where the writer put them, numbered from 1. The book is one group now: `numbering: group` numbers its notes across its chapter files, as the book's one page numbers them, `placement: group` gathers them at the end of the last, and `placement: book` on the Notes chapter.
- **Notes gathered from a group's pages kept each page's numbers.** With `notes.placement: group` or `book` and `notes.numbering: page`, the default, the notes of a chapter cut into pages landed in one list numbered as each page had them, two of them 1 and their ids the same, which the output check reported; the EPUB target `docs/configuration.md` gives as an example, `placement: book` alone, did it to every chapter cut into pages. Notes gathered in one list are now numbered across their group whatever `notes.numbering` says, as the description of `book` said they were.
- **A note's code lost its colors and line numbers where `notes.placement` moved it, and could share its ids.** Pandoc writes the CSS for highlighted code only on a page that holds code when it writes it, and counts each page's code from `cb1`, so a note with code gathered on a group's last page or the Notes page, on a page or in an EPUB, had no CSS for it there, and two notes' code, or a note's and the page's own, had the same ids. A moved note's code has ids named for the note now (`g1-fn3-cb1`), and the page it lands on gets the CSS where Pandoc puts it on a page with code.
- **The output check reported a note's way back as a link whose label leaves out its text**, an error on every page whose notes were arranged by `notes.numbering: group` or a `notes.placement` other than `page`: the `↩︎` labeled "Back to reference 3". Text used as a symbol, with no letter or digit in it, isn't a visible label to WCAG 2.5.3 ([W3C's Understanding document](https://www.w3.org/WAI/WCAG21/Understanding/label-in-name.html) says so), so such a link isn't reported; one whose text is words or a number still is.
- **A PDF built to no PDF/UA standard stopped the run.** With `pdf.standard` naming none (`[]`, or a PDF/A part alone), LaTeX tags nothing, and the tables' repair after it stopped on the missing structure tree (`AttributeError: /StructTreeRoot`), the PDF written and the run saying it wasn't. The repairs, which only a tagged PDF needs, are left out of an untagged one, and the output check says it isn't tagged.
- **A book's PDF declared no passage's language.** A span, a link, or a div with `lang` was marked for babel, which sets a passage's hyphenation and tags nothing, so the PDF had a language in its catalog alone, where PDF/UA-1 wants every change of language declared (ISO 14289-1, 7.2) and a screen reader reads a passage in the language it's given. `pdf-target.lua` tags a span or a link a Span with its `/Lang`, and a div's blocks with the div's language, as a deck's PDF tags them, a heading in the div by its words alone so its section isn't taken for the passage's; babel's commands stay inside them, and the pages look as they did. The `latex` target writes the same, and reads back without it.
- **A row of `math-keep.csv` was warned about when its setting was off.** With `math.repair_equations` off in every target, a row keeping an equation kept nothing, and the run said the math it named wasn't in the book; such a row, and with `math.from_text` off one keeping text, now isn't named, since it couldn't have kept anything, and the warning says a row's math may be there with nothing for the repairs to change, which keeps nothing too. And a slides run's summary counted a deck changed when all it did was leave a slide's own title as it was.
- **Highlighted code in a LaTeX source was read as a paragraph, or as nothing, and inline highlighted code stopped the reading.** Pandoc's LaTeX writer, the `latex` target's, and R Markdown's and Quarto's with it, puts a highlighted block in fancyvrb's `Highlighting` environment, each token in a macro of its own, and inline code in `\VERB`, neither of which Pandoc's reader takes: a block came back a paragraph of words in colors no browser reads (nothing, where the preamble doesn't define the macros), and inline code holding a bracket or a parenthesis stopped the reading of the whole book ("unexpected ()"). The copy the reader reads has each as the code it shows now, a block numbered as it was and with its id; and the `latex` target writes a code block's language in a comment before it (`% TextbookImproverCode python`), as a Word file's bookmark keeps it, so it reads back highlighted. An inline code's language isn't carried. `util/compare-output.py`, by which the suite's round trip of the `latex` target is judged, doesn't compare code, so the suite checks its book's code apart, every character of ASCII in it.
- **Highlighted code was in colors with too little contrast.** Pandoc highlights code in its pygments style unless told otherwise, and 13 of the style's 28 colored kinds of token are under WCAG's 4.5:1 on a page (SC 1.4.3), in six colors: a comment's `#60a0b0` is 2.89:1 on Pandoc's `#fdfdfd`, and numbers, attributes, preprocessor lines, special strings, errors, and alerts fall short too, as does a numbered line's number (`#aaaaaa`, 2.28:1). Every writer is given `bin/highlighting.theme` now: pygments, each of those colors darkened to 4.6:1 or more, its hue and chroma kept, so a page, an EPUB, a PDF, a Word file, a PowerPoint file, and a reveal.js presentation have the same colors, a comment `#3c7c8c`. The dark mode Pandoc 3.12's stylesheets follow leaves the colors as they are, on a page's `#1a1a1a` and an EPUB's `#232629`, where a variable's `#19177c` is 1.21:1 and 1.05:1, so `page.css` gives each kind of token a lighter color there, 4.6:1 or more on the EPUB's background, the lighter of the two; a browser or reading system without `light-dark()` keeps the theme's. The suites check each output's colors, and `page.css`'s against the backgrounds the stylesheets Pandoc writes give.
- **The advice for an equation image named Word whatever the source.** A picture of an equation whose alt text is MathSpeak, its letters rejoined, drew "consider re-authoring this as a real equation in the DOCX", from an HTML page, a Markdown file, or a book unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML too (*Introduction to Business 2e*, *Principles of Management*, *Principles of Financial Accounting*). It says "in the source" now, naming a Word equation, TeX math, and MathML, and so does the troubleshooting page.
- **A run stopped reading a report's rows back when a quotation in one was cut inside a character.** The excerpt of a table that needs a description (`table-captions-missing.csv`), and what `fidelity.csv` quotes of a footnote or an example list, were cut at a count of bytes, which can end inside a character of several (a minus sign is three), and `convert.py` stopped with a UnicodeDecodeError reading the row: *Elementary Algebra 2e*, unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML. They're cut at a count of characters now.
- **A link whose scheme was in capitals was taken for a file of the book's.** `Http://www.pwc.com/…`, as *Introduction to Intellectual Property*'s source writes an address, was a missing file to the output check (`link-to-missing-file`), and the packager stopped the run ("referenced file(s) are not on disk"); a picture at an `Http://` address stopped it at the check for missing media; and the audit didn't take a link whose text is its own address for a bare link when the scheme was in capitals (`source-link-bare-url`). A scheme is compared in lowercase now, as a browser reads it.
- **A split table's part laid out on fewer columns than the table spanned columns that began no cell of it.** *Foundations of Information Systems*' Table 3.7 is three tables in normal form, Orders on its four columns and Customers and Addresses on two each, their cells across two; split at its bands, the Customers and Addresses parts each had two columns no cell began in, invalid HTML (the Nu checker's "Table column 2 established by element th has no cells beginning in it", 4 errors on its page, unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML). A part's columns that no cell of it begins in are closed up now, a cell across one spanning one column fewer and the column's width going to the one before it.
- **A Markdown target lost the groups of a table whose groups each open with a header row of their own.** Kept whole for a source target (`tables.bands: group`), such a table, the shape the guess splits at a row like the first but for its first cell, has a body per group headed by its row, and Pandoc's Markdown writer made those rows ordinary ones of a single body: *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s Table 7.2, unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML, was a pipe table with an empty header row, and read back, it had no headers and no groups. It's written as HTML now, as a table with merged cells is and as an AsciiDoc target writes it, and reads back with its groups and their rows, which split it again for HTML into the parts the book's own HTML has: such a `<tbody>`'s head counts as a band now, as a head one cell across already did.
- **A table split at its bands repeated ids in its parts.** The header row each part repeats kept the ids it and its cells had in the source; each part after the first repeated the table's caption with any ids in it; and a part headed by a header row of its own, a group's name in its corner, took the corner's words into its caption with their ids, which the row kept too. So a page held such an id more than once: invalid HTML (the Nu checker's "Duplicate ID"), and an EPUB whose chapter holds such a table fails epubcheck (RSC-005). A Word table has no ids, but an HTML source's can, and a book unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML keeps its source's: the Polish *Mikroekonomia – Podstawy* and *Makroekonomia - Podstawy* had 38 such errors on their pages and 58 in their EPUBs. The first part keeps the ids, which a link to the row, a cell, or the caption names, and each part's own header row keeps its own; what's repeated has none, and an anchor that held nothing but an id goes with it.
- **With Ubuntu 24.04's pypdf, 4.0.2, a LaTeX book's PDF figures kept the fonts they don't embed.** pypdf before 4.1 doesn't follow a reference to another object in what it reads, and Ghostscript lists a figure's fonts by one (`/Font 7 0 R`), so the check that a figure written again had its fonts embedded failed every time: the run warned that the figures couldn't be written with their fonts embedded, kept them as they were, and the book's PDF failed PDF/UA. It follows the reference now, and so does the output check, the audit's too, which had passed over the fonts of a page that lists them that way. The suites failed with it as well: the PDF suite stopped on the same thing in its own reading (`'IndirectObject' object is not iterable`), and three of the decks suite's checks looked for text as pypdf 5.1 and later extract it, where an earlier release puts a space where the font changes ("per unit ."); they leave spaces out of the comparison now. Every suite passes with pypdf 4.0.2 and pikepdf 8.7.1 on Python 3.12, as Ubuntu 24.04 packages them, and with pypdf 5.9 and pikepdf 10.13. And `build-cartridge.py --toc` without pypdf says to install it with `apt`, as the installation page does, where it said `pip3 install pypdf`, which Ubuntu 24.04 refuses.
- **A `tlmgr` command the run suggested for a TeX Live installed with `sudo` wasn't found.** On Ubuntu, `sudo` looks for programs in a list of folders of its own, which TeX Live's isn't in, so `sudo tlmgr install preview` answered `sudo: tlmgr: command not found`, or ran Ubuntu's own `tlmgr` where its `texlive` packages are installed too. The run names `tlmgr` by its path after `sudo` now (`sudo /usr/local/texlive/2026/bin/x86_64-linux/tlmgr install preview`), which is all it needs, since `tlmgr` puts its own folder first on the path for the programs it runs. And the installation page installs TeX Live as you, into a `/usr/local/texlive` made yours first, as the TeX Live project recommends installing it, so that its `tlmgr` needs no `sudo`, as TinyTeX's doesn't; says how to make one installed with `sudo` yours, or how to give `sudo` the path; says that a full TeX Live needs none of its `tlmgr install` lines; and writes those lines a command each, where a command continued over four lines could run two names together when copied (`package soultabto-ltx not present in repository`).
- **A table split at its bands headed every part with its first band where that band was the table's head.** Pandoc's Word reader makes a table's first row its head when Word's table look formats the first row, as it does in a table Word inserts, along with the rows after it marked to repeat, and an HTML table's first band can be its `<thead>`. The split copied the head into every part, so the first band headed them all ("Example A" over Example B's rows), the first part was captioned "Part 1", and where each part had its own header row under its band, the shape of *Principles of Economics 3e*'s cost tables, those rows were left as data. Such a band heads the first part alone now, and so does a header row under it in the head that's the first part's own, where a band below has a header row of its own or rows with their cells elsewhere. Over a header row every part shares, the band is the table's title, which the guess makes a caption row, and the header row alone heads every part, where the band had headed them all with it. Five tables in the Word corpus that are split at their bands have one in their head, and all five come out right now: *Foundations of Information Systems*'s Tables 3.5 to 3.7, each part headed by its own header row, which had been data under the first band; *Clinical Nursing Skills*'s Table 12.2, whose second part, needle gauges, had been headed by the first part's band and header row, needle lengths, over its own; and its Table 4.4, whose title had headed both parts.
- **A title over a header row was taken for the table's first band where nothing marked the header row.** A merged first row over a row that reads as a header row (every cell bold or shaded, or words over numbers), with the first band right under that, was named in `split-at`: the first part held the header row alone, as a row of data, no other part had one, and the guess for the table's headers, reading the parts without it, said `first-column` for a table of answers counted by group. The merged row is the table's title now, a caption row, the header row heads every part, and the guess reads each part under it (`both` for that table). Where a band below has a header row of its own, or rows with their cells elsewhere, the merged row is still the first band. No table in the Word corpus has the shape.
- **An HTML table whose head was its title alone made its first row of data its header row.** A `<thead>` holding one cell across the table declared a header row, and once the title had gone to the caption (`caption-rows`), the row under it was made one. A head row one cell across the table is its title or a band now, which declares no header row.
- **A table split at its bands lost its foot.** An HTML table's `<tfoot>`, which holds its notes or sources (OpenStax's *Principles of Management* keeps its tables' sources there), was left off every part the split made, and off the table kept whole with a body per band (`tables.bands: group`) or given a column for its bands (`column`), since tables were first split at their bands. The foot comes after the last part now, and the table kept whole keeps it, a row one cell across spanning the bands' column too; and the guess names no row of it in `split-at`.
- **The text after a table whose label stood above it was lost.** A table with no caption of its own takes the label paragraph beside it for its caption, above it or below, and after one taken from above, the filter passed over the block after the table as if it were a label below, and dropped it, whatever it was: the paragraph after such a table went missing, in every release so far, or the heading or the table after it, and where two tables had only their labels between them, the second one's label went, and that table was reported by its place, uncaptioned. The filter suite's own document lost the four paragraphs between its tables, which no check looked for. It takes nothing after a table whose label is above it now. A table with a caption of its own (an HTML `<caption>`, a Markdown caption line, or a Word paragraph in a caption style, which Pandoc's reader attaches to its table) wasn't touched, nor one whose label is below it.
- **A link to another site lost its fragment's spaces and escapes in an HTML source.** The repair that takes the spaces out of an id, and makes each link to it follow, rewrote every link's `#fragment`, another site's too: a text fragment (`#:~:text=Now%20worth%20a%20cool%20%242.2,96%25`) was decoded and hyphenated (`Now-worth-a-cool-$2.2,96%`), which finds nothing on that page, and its lone `%` made the address invalid, which the Nu HTML checker and epubcheck reject (*Introduction to Political Science*'s references, unpacked from OpenStax's CNXML). A link that names a scheme (`https:`) or a host (`//example.org/…`) is left as it is now. A link to an id here has its spaces made hyphens, written or escaped (`%20`), as before, and its escaped letters decoded, a space among them or not (`#na%C3%AFve` is `#naïve`), since pages split from a source and an EPUB's chapters find an id by the fragment as written: such a link from another page, with no space in it, pointed into its own chapter in an EPUB, which epubcheck reports (RSC-012). Any other escape stays as written, where a `%25` beside a space had become a lone `%` too. The output check finds a fragment's id as written or else decoded, as a browser does, where it had reported a link with an escaped letter as going nowhere.
- **Math typed as text was shown as its TeX where an equation's command came before a Greek letter.** With `math.from_text`, *Chemistry 2e*'s *q* = *m* × *c* × Δ*T*, its × an equation of its own in the source, was made the equation `q=m\times c\timesΔT`, which texmath, reading any letter as part of a command's name, took for a command it doesn't know (`\timesΔT`), though LuaLaTeX reads `\times` and then Δ: the page and the EPUB showed the TeX, and the output check reported it (`formula-shown-as-tex`). A command is now set apart from a character that isn't ASCII, as it was from a letter.
- **A table lost rows where splitting it at its bands left a part with none.** The split that makes a table one per band (`tables.bands: split`, the default for HTML, EPUB, and PDF) dropped a band with no row under it before the next or the table's end. The guess took a note one cell across a table's foot for a band, so the note went, and a table with no other band was captioned "Part 1": the conclusion of *Principles of Economics 3e*'s Table 11.1 ("Then the four-firm concentration ratio is 16 + 10 + 8 + 6 = 40.") and the last rows of five more tables in the Word corpus. And it took every row of a graphic organizer, a bold "Criterion N" beside the same bold list in each (the *Writing Guide*'s Table 15.2), for a group's header, as a bold row repeating row 1 but for its first cell is in the sociology book's Table 7.2, so the table went whole, without a word. A band with nothing but bands under it to the table's end, guessed or declared in `table-headers.csv`, stays a row of the table now, as does a group's header row right over another band, and the guess names no such band or group. A row declared in `split-at` that the table isn't split at is named in the run's output, and so is a `part-captions` that gives more or fewer captions than the table has parts. Two more shapes lost a row the same way, neither in the corpus: a merged first row right over a band is the table's title now, which the guess makes a caption row, and a band right over another, a group of groups, goes into the caption of the part below with it ("Table 3.2: Part Two: Group B"); where a table has such a pair partway down, a pair at its top is one too, not a title over a band.
- **The `source` target's copy of a LaTeX book stopped LaTeX where the keys it writes weren't known.** Without tagging, a tikzpicture's `alt` or `artifact` stopped pdfLaTeX with any pgf before 3.1.12 (2026-08-01), which defines both as keys that do nothing, where only tagging's support for TikZ had ("I do not know the key '/tikz/alt'"); a picture's stopped any LaTeX before 2026-06-01, whose `\picture` took no options; and `artifact` on an image stopped TeX Live 2023's graphicx. The copy's master has a block before `\begin{document}` now that gives such a key one that does nothing and leaves a known one alone: checked with TeX Live 2023, 2025, and 2026, a picture without keys drawn as before, and, tagged, every figure keeping its alt text. GIAM's copy, a placeholder for each of its 104 images and drawings, builds to its 434 pages on all three, where 2025's stopped at its pictures before. The suite's book has a tikzpicture now, and the suite builds the block against the older definitions, where a document using every key stops without it, whatever the TeX that runs it: on a TeX Live 2026 brought up to date, the book's copy builds without the block too.
- **A table marked unnumbered that has a caption stopped a PDF's LaTeX** ("No float type 'none' defined"). Pandoc writes such a table's caption with `\LTcaptype` set to `none`, and the caption package, which its template loads, looks for the list such a table goes in. OpenStax marks the tables it doesn't number so, and the run gives every table a caption, so no OpenStax book unpacked from its CNXML built as a PDF; a Markdown table can be marked so too (`Table: Cost by year {.unnumbered}`). Its caption is set without a number now, and listed nowhere.
- **The circled letters OpenStax's math books letter a question's parts with (ⓐ, ⓑ) were drawn blank in a PDF**, 1,391 of them in the first chapter of *College Algebra*: neither Latin Modern font nor DejaVu Sans has them. New Computer Modern's math font, installed wherever decks are built, comes last among the fonts the build falls back to now, when it's installed.
- **A code block in a footnote stopped a PDF's LaTeX**, a book's or a deck's ("Paragraph ended before \FV@BeginScanning was complete"): LaTeX's tagged footnote takes its text as an argument, where a code block's verbatim can't be read, and fancyvrb's `\VerbatimFootnotes`, which Pandoc's template gives such a book, doesn't reach it. Each code block in a footnote is now written to a file of its own from the preamble and read where it stood when the note is set (`bin/footnote-code.lua`), highlighted as a code block anywhere else is, its lines and spaces kept. Highlighted code in a deck's section's footnote, which is written apart from the deck, stopped LaTeX too, on the highlighting macros a deck with no other highlighted code didn't have; such a deck is given them.
- **A heading the split cut at lost its footnote**, and its emphasis and formula: a piece's H1 was the heading's words alone, so the note was on no page. The H1 is the heading as written now, its footnote's text on its page, and a heading with nothing of its own but a footnote, which had been a group no page showed, is a page.
- **A frame sized in a fraction of a pixel was given a style no browser takes.** An HTML page's `<iframe height="371.4">`, as OpenStax's books size every frame, became `style="height: 371.4"`, which the Nu HTML checker rejects as a length without a unit, and the frame lost its height. Such a size is rounded to whole pixels now (`371`), as `1200px` loses its unit.
- **The run said a page's title stood without its heading's number when the two were the same**: "142 page(s) keep their declared title without the number their heading puts before it ("1.1 What Is Economics, and Why Is It Important?" is titled "1.1 What Is Economics, and Why Is It Important?")", for a book whose pages are titled with their numbers. It says so only when the title is the heading without the number.
- **A table whose own caption is its label alone got no description.** An HTML page's `<caption>Table 3.8</caption>`, a Markdown table's `Table: Table 3.8`, or a Word table's caption paragraph reading only "Table 3.8" (Pandoc's reader takes a paragraph in a caption style beside a table for its caption) names the table and says nothing of it, and `table-captions.csv` keys on that label, but the sidecar was read only for a label in a paragraph beside a table, so such a table wasn't described, and wasn't reported in `table-captions-missing.csv` either: 62 of *Principles of Economics 3e*'s tables, as HTML. It's described from the sidecar and reported without a row now, as a label paragraph is, and a `source` target's copy joins the description to the caption: an HTML page's `<caption>`, a Markdown table's caption line (`Table:`, `table:`, or `:`, before any attributes it ends with), or a Word file's caption paragraph, before the table or after it.
- **A link to a table that the run split into parts died.** `tables.bands: split`, the default for HTML, EPUB, and PDF, numbered the parts' ids from the table's own (`costs-1`, `costs-2`), so nothing on the page had the id a link to the table names (`#costs`): in *Principles of Economics 3e*, as HTML whose tables have ids, 12 links to 6 tables went nowhere. The first part keeps the table's id now, and the others are numbered from 2.
- **A title a Markdown source's YAML gives as a list of one was lost.** `title:` with `- The Title` on the next line, as half the decks this was found in give theirs, is a list to Pandoc's writers, which titled the page by its intermediate's file name (`S30-Money.filtered`); a title, subtitle, date, or language given so is now read as the value.
- **An image a source names in another case than its file's stopped the run**, at the media gate (`assets/cs168-layers.png`, the file `CS168-layers.png`), though macOS and Windows find it, and so does TeX Live on Linux. A Markdown, HTML, or AsciiDoc source's image is now read as its file, named in a warning, as one named with Windows's backslashes is, and a `source` target's copy of a Markdown or HTML page takes the image's alt text from the sidecar's row for the file.
- **A footnote in a definition list's term stopped a PDF's LaTeX**, with LaTeX's tagging counting a paragraph's hooks unequal (`The number of automatic begin (2) and end (3) text`): the writer gives such a footnote as a mark in the term and its text after the list, which LaTeX 2026-06-01 can't tag in a list's label. It goes at the head of the term's definition now, where the writer gives it as a footnote.
- **Highlighted code in a book's PDF lost its spaces to a screen reader.** Pandoc sets highlighted code in fancyvrb's `Verbatim` (inline, `\Verb`), its words colored with `\textcolor`, and under LuaLaTeX a space beside a color's change was no character in the PDF's text, and a run of spaces one: a screen reader got `return12-2* p >0` and lost every indentation. Each space made as LaTeX's own verbatim makes one (`\nobreak\ `) puts them back, and nothing on the page moves; a deck's PDF has the same. Found in a deck's PDF.
- **A page whose source sets a link color had CSS the Nu checker stopped on**: Pandoc 3.12's stylesheet writes `light-dark(blue, inherit)` for `linkcolor: blue`, and `inherit` is no color. A link takes the text's own color in dark mode now (`currentcolor`), as it would with no link color.
- **The bar the math repair wrote into a Word file was silent in Narrator.** A `format: source` copy, and `remediate.py --repair-equations`, wrote x̄ as the accent Pandoc writes for `\bar`, whose character is an overline (U+203E), and Narrator read it as plain "cap X", in Word as in PowerPoint; LibreOffice 24.2 draws that accent as an acute accent. It's an accent of the macron (U+00AF) now, which Narrator read as "cap X overbar" and NVDA, with MathCAT, as "X bar", and which LibreOffice reads and writes as its own bar accent. Read back by a conversion, the copy gives `\bar{x}`, as the pages have it. And a bar written inside another's base, which was left as it was, is repaired with it.
- **A bar in a Word file the `docx` target writes was silent in Narrator, and an acute accent in LibreOffice.** It was the accent Pandoc writes for `\bar`, of the overline (U+203E); the target gives it the macron (U+00AF), as the math repair now writes a bar, and the run counts them. `\overline` is Office's overbar, which the target leaves. Read back as a source, the file still gives `\bar{x}`.
- **A bare link's text Replacement became its address in a `source` copy**, for Word, HTML, and Markdown sources alike: a row giving `The Data and Story Library's cars data` as a Replacement, which [Bare links](docs/bare-links.md) says replaces only a link's text, made it the link's text and its `href` (or a Word relationship's `Target`), a link to nowhere, where the pages from the filter had it right. An address in a Replacement still replaces both. Found while writing the same decisions into PowerPoint decks.
- **An image marked `Decorative`, or `[DECORATIVE]`, in the image-alt sidecar got those words as its alt text in a `format: source` copy.** The filter took the marker in any case, so the pages had the image decorative, but the code writing the decisions into a book's Word, HTML, Markdown, and LaTeX files took only `[decorative]` as written. Both read it from one list now, held to the filter's by a unit check.
- **The LaTeX, convert, and PDF suites ran with a Pandoc too old for `convert.py`**, which refuses one older than 3.9, so every case failed with the reason only in the run's own output: seven of one LaTeX case's checks, run by hand with Ubuntu 24.04's Pandoc 3.1.3. Each stops first now and says so, as the filter, EPUB, and split suites did, and the unpacking suite's one case that converts skips; a unit check holds every suite that runs `convert.py` to the same minimum.
- **pypdf's warnings in the middle of the output check**, as it read past a fault in a PDF's syntax: GIAM's PDF printed "Multiple definitions in dictionary … for key /Group" twice, for two figures LaTeX's graphics code gives a second `/Group`. They aren't findings, and veraPDF judges the file; the PDF check reads without them now, as the figure check did. And without pypdf, it says how to install it on Ubuntu 22.04 too.
- **The output check's summary left the Nu checker's line undescribed** (`62  vnu:error: `, on GIAM), since its descriptions held only the checks of its own; a validator's line says what it is now ("the Nu HTML checker reported an error").
- **An equation holding middle dots as text was shown as TeX.** The statistics book's geometric mean, `(x₁ · x₂ ··· xₙ)`, has its ellipsis in normal text, `\text{···}` to Pandoc, and the equation repair made each dot `\cdot`, which text can't hold: Pandoc couldn't convert the equation, warned on every run, and put its TeX in the page as `$r_{s} = \left( …`. Text inside an equation gets only `μ` and `Δ` now, the repairs of one character for another, and a Word source's copy leaves a `ŷ` in normal text as typed, as the page does.
- **A page's line about the raw HTML tags its reader dropped named a temporary file** (`/tmp/convert-…/repaired-html/glossary.html`), the repaired copy Pandoc reads. It names the page's file now.
- **`build-cartridge.py --init` wrote the defaults, not the book's settings**, since it read no configuration: a sample renamed over `packaging.yaml` lost everything set there. It reads `project.yaml` and `packaging.yaml` now, as a run does, and writes the sample from them: the project block only as `packaging.yaml` holds it, its contents as declared, each package's settings in its own block, never a chosen package's as everyone's defaults, and `project-sample.yaml` beside it only when nothing names the book and no run has written one, which would have what `--init` can't know. It writes its samples with no pages to read, rather than stopping at "No .html files" or a missing `html/`, which the documented `-d . --init` did on any book whose pages are in `html/`. Like `read-conversion-config.py --init`, which resolves its targets the same way now, it no longer refuses a configuration of several targets.
- **`read-conversion-config.py --init`'s HTML target wrote its pages beside the sources** (`output_dir: .`), the layout before 0.4; it writes them into `html/` now, as a run without `conversion.yaml` does.
- **The settings reference pages described v0.1's layout**, conversion settings "under `conversion:` in `packaging.yaml`" and the project's "at the top level of `packaging.yaml`". Each now says which file its settings are in and how to write that file's sample, and [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#samples) lists all three.
- **Checks that couldn't fail.** The audit's check that the source check and the filter agree about images without alt text looked for a summary line the filter no longer prints, one line an image now; its check that an EPUB is audited was never given one, so the audit's EPUB path went unchecked; its headerless table wasn't in its fixture, so the check accepted none. The EPUB suite never kept the stylesheet its check looked in, the check suite's note about a missing validator passed wherever both were installed, and the convert suite's two groups from one source were two sources. Each now fails when what it guards breaks. The spelling check knew neither "sanitise" nor "tokenise", one of each in the suites, and it reads the changelog's released sections now too, whose one slip, in 0.5's notes, is fixed.
- **The LaTeX suite passed when `--case` named no case.** It takes any part of a case's label, and given a part no label holds it ran nothing and said its checks passed; it stops now, naming the part.
- **Every run of the suites left four folders in the temporary folder**, the PDF suite's PDF/UA-1 book and three of the unit suite's; both remove theirs now.
- **A page's notes, renumbered or gathered, went after its footer.** Where `notes.numbering` or `notes.placement` moves them, they were put at the end of the page's `<body>`, after the `footer` setting's attribution line; they go at the end of the page's own content now, inside `<main>`.
- **A target's header and footer took the place of a page's own `include-before` and `include-after`**, an epigraph a Markdown source gives in its metadata, say, since Pandoc's options for them replace the page's, and a reveal.js presentation's script took the place of a deck's own `include-after`. The page has both now, its own inside its main content, and the presentation has the deck's, the script after it.
- **A fill-in blank ran past the end of its line in a PDF.** TeX can't break a run of underscores, so a question ending in a blank too long for what's left of its line ran into the margin, one in *Introduction to Sociology 3e*'s chapter 19 quiz by 24 points. A run of four or more underscores is now drawn shorter where it, or text joined to its end, as in `(____)`, runs past its line's end, its underscores overlapping, so the line ends where it should and its spaces are their natural width; every underscore is still drawn, and a blank that fits is left as it is. A blank longer than two thirds of a ragged line shares it with the words before it that fit, as a form's label does, TeX asked to end the line after it, or after a formula joined to it; and a blank drawn shorter, or drawn to fill its line, isn't hung into the margin, as microtype hangs an underscore at a line's start or end. A blank is never drawn shorter than three underscores, and underscores in code, a link's text, a heading, or a picture's description are left alone, as is a blank underlined, struck out, or highlighted. The build no longer counts such a line among those wider than the text. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A table written in HTML in a Markdown source lost its table where a cell held a note**, its cells' text run together, in every output: the cells went through Pandoc's HTML writer to be read as HTML, and the writer puts a note's text at the end of what it writes, a `<section>` inside the table, which the HTML reader made no table of. A note is held in its place while the table is read now, and put back, so the table is a table and its note a note in its cell, a Markdown target's table too. [Raw HTML](docs/markdown.md#raw-html).
- **A raw `<pre>` in a Markdown source was raw HTML, in the pages and the EPUB and left out of a PDF.** Pandoc's Markdown reader keeps one that opens a block whole, and the filter had left it as written. It's a code block now, its lines and spaces as written, but a line end right after its tag, which HTML drops; one without a `<code>` inside is given one, as Pandoc's HTML reader has read a bare `<pre>` as paragraphs in the text's face since 3.11, where 3.9 read it as a code block. [Raw HTML](docs/markdown.md#raw-html).
- **A PDF's table counted a note's mark as 0.8 of an em in any face**, two of IBM Plex Serif's digits at 12 points, where LaTeX sets the mark of two 0.82 wide there, the space after a superscript included, and 0.79 in Latin Modern; and a mark of three digits, in a book of 100 notes or more, or a symbol wider than two digits, as `\fnsymbol` numbers notes, was counted as two digits. The build measures the mark with the rest of the page, in the book's faces and as it numbers its notes (`measure-page.lua`): the widest of the first nine notes' marks and of one digit, and a digit's advance more for each digit past the first the book's notes need, so that a book whose notes are "(1)" to "(99)" has each counted as wide as LaTeX sets it, and a book of nine notes or fewer has marks of one. A book whose own `\@makefnmark` sets every mark in a box of one width, a digit adding nothing, is measured too. A word with a note's mark after it in a table's cell is given the room LaTeX sets them in. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A Markdown copy's table description went under the wrong table, or after the table outside its list item or quotation.** The `source` target writes a table's description from the table-captions sidecar into a Markdown file as a `Table:` line after a table that has none, found by its place among the page's tables; but the filter, which records the place, doesn't number a note's table, and numbers a table written in HTML as the pages read it, as many tables as its header rows split it into, or none, where the copy had counted every table Pandoc's Markdown reader reads: after a note's table, a description went to the table before its own, and after one written in HTML, to the table after its own, without a word. And the line was written at the margin, where after a table in a list item, a definition, or a block quote Pandoc reads it as a paragraph after the list or the quote, its table still without a caption. The copy counts the tables the filter numbers, in its order, now; writes none for a table written in HTML, in a raw block of any kind, or a table after one, and the run says how many; writes the line as far in as the table's rule is, inside the quote; and takes out again a line Pandoc doesn't read back as its table's caption, as after a table in a div that has a paragraph after it, and counts it. [Source](docs/formats.md#source).
- **A line break's two lines ran together in a tagged PDF's text**, the last word of one and the first of the next one word to a screen reader: NVDA 2026.3 in Acrobat read an address set with line breaks as "OpenStaxRice University6100 Main Street", a word at a time too, and a poem's lines as "dreary,While". LaTeX's `\\` and `\newline` end a line with no space, and LaTeX's tagging writes a space at a line's end only where TeX broke the line at one. A line break in a book's PDF, in a paragraph, a line block, a caption, a list item or its term, a note, or a link, now leaves a space between its lines in the text a screen reader reads, copies, and searches, and nothing on the page moves; but a line ending in a hyphen joined to its word gets none, so "self-" and "contained" stay one word, where a dash with a space before it keeps its space, and neither does a line block's empty line, nor a line before one indented with spaces of its own. So do a LaTeX book's `\\`, `\newline`, `\linebreak`, `\break`, and `\hfill\break` in its tagged copy built with LuaLaTeX, and a `\linebreak[2]` where TeX breaks the line at it, and the run says so; with pdfLaTeX, a tagged PDF's text has no space at the end of any line. A slide's lines, and a table cell's in a table with column widths, which LaTeX sets as paragraphs of their own, were apart already; in a table without widths, Pandoc's writer set a cell's lines as boxes, which LaTeX's tagging left out of the cell (below). [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A numbered chapter's heading ran its number into its title in a tagged PDF's text**, "Chapter 1Introduction", and a chapter title's lines into each other, "Chapter onesecond line": the book class sets the number as a paragraph of its own, and a title's line break ends a paragraph, and LaTeX's tagging made each paragraph a piece of the heading with nothing between them. A part's heading did the same. They read with a space between them now, in a book's PDF and in a LaTeX book's tagged copy, which gets the space where it numbers a part or a chapter, and the run says so. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A running head ran a title's two lines together on the page**: a chapter "Chapter one" with a line break before "second line" had "CHAPTER ONESECOND LINE" across the top of its pages, where the line break set nothing. It's a space in the head now.
- **A LaTeX book's heading lost its line break from its bookmark and its title in the PDF's structure**, which read "Section witha break"; the break is a space there now, as Pandoc's writer gives a Markdown book's heading one already.
- **A table whose columns have no widths left a cell holding a line break empty to a screen reader.** Pandoc's writer set such a cell's lines as boxes, which LaTeX's tagging leaves out of the cell, its text outside the PDF's structure (a Markdown table's cells holding `<br>`). The table is given the equal widths the writer set its columns at, as Pandoc's HTML reader gives a table like it, so each of a cell's lines is a paragraph of the cell, and it's set as a table with widths is: a column narrower than its longest word is widened to hold it, and the header's cells are set from their last lines, as Pandoc's writer sets them. And a table inside a table's cell, which the PDF sets in columns that don't wrap, sets a line break as a space, where it had ended the row: a cell "Inner" with a line break before "break" was two rows' cells. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A caption short enough for one line but for a line break was spread across the line** in a book's PDF, "Figure 2.1: Short", 79 points of space, then "caption": LaTeX's tagging sets such a caption in a box as wide as the line, where the line break's own fill took a third of the line's room, and untagged, LaTeX ran the two words together. It's two lines now, broken at the line break and centered, as the caption package sets a table's caption, in a book's PDF and a LaTeX book's tagged copy; OpenStax's books put a figure's credit on a line of its own so. A line break at a caption's end is nothing. And a list's term, which LaTeX sets in a box that can't break, has a space's width before a line break's fill, where its words ran together on the page, "Termtwo", as does an `\mbox` in a LaTeX book's tagged copy; a box set to a width shares its room as before. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A contents entry's number, title, and page number ran together in a tagged PDF's text**, "1.1A section3". They're a space apart now, and nothing on the page moves, in the contents and a list of figures or tables, in a book's PDF and a LaTeX book's tagged copy, which gets the space where it sets such a list in the article, report, or book class, and the run says so; memoir's, KOMA-Script's, and amsbook's contents are their classes' own and still run together. So is a LaTeX book's `\enspace` in text, a kern, for which LaTeX's tagging writes no space, amsbook's chapter heading among them, which read "CHAPTER1", and in a heading's title in the PDF's structure, which read "BetaGamma". [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A note in a link's text was an anchor inside the link's in the pages**, which HTML doesn't allow, as Pandoc's writer writes it, and in a PDF it was inside the link's structure element, its text read as part of the link. The note goes after the link now, in every format but a Markdown or AsciiDoc copy, which keeps what the author wrote; several go in their order. And a link whose text was a note alone went, as a link with no text does, and took the note with it, in every format: the note stays where it was now. [Formats](docs/formats.md#pdf).
- **A note in a LaTeX book's caption took two numbers in its tagged copy.** LaTeX's tagging sets a caption twice, once in a box to measure it and once on the page, and `\footnotemark` counted both times, so with `\caption{...\protect\footnotemark}` and `\footnotetext` after the figure, a chapter's first such note was numbered 2, the next 4, and a footnote after them 5. That's a float's caption, a `longtable`'s, a `\captionof`, and the caption package's `\captionbox`; in a minipage, a `\footnote` in a `\captionof` took two letters and its text was set twice under it. The counters and the minipage's notes are set back after the caption is measured, so the notes are 1, 2, and 3, and the run says how many captions hold a note's mark. A Markdown book's PDF had this already. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#the-source-target).
- **An amsbook book's tagged copy made each list a numbered chapter.** amsbook starts its contents and its lists of figures and tables with its own `\chapter`, told to set nothing but the new page, and LaTeX's tagging puts a `\chapter` of its own in amsbook's place, which took the list's next commands for a title: each list's heading was "CHAPTER 1" with "sec/title/end" printed under it, the book's chapters were numbered one more for each list, and LaTeX ended with an error. Each list starts its page as amsbook's `\chapter` starts it now, in the copy and in a Markdown book whose class is amsbook, and the run says so. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#a-classs-own-contents).
- **An amsart or amsproc book with a contents couldn't be built tagged.** Those classes head each list with a sectioning command of no name and a level LaTeX's tagging has no name for, and LaTeX stopped on it, with 14 errors for a contents ("Missing number", "Undefined control sequence"), so the PDF target stopped. Each list's heading is an unnumbered section now, set with the class's own spacing and type, in the tagged copy and in a Markdown book whose class is one of them, and the run says so; untagged, the class's own heading is left. [LaTeX input](docs/latex.md#a-classs-own-contents).
- **A Markdown book in an AMS class printed "TextbookImprover list end" in its PDF.** The PDF's LaTeX marks where each list is set in the log, to name a line too wide in it as the list's, and took the list's command, `\@starttoc`, to have LaTeX's one argument, where amsart, amsbook, and amsproc give it two, the list's heading the second: with `documentclass: amsbook` and a contents, the measuring run and the build stopped on four errors, the contents had no heading, and the marker's words were on the page after it. The marker takes the arguments the class's command takes now, and leaves a command defined some other way alone.
- **A numbered book's page whose title held a character outside ASCII, a formula, or a note had no number in its `<title>` or its heading**, where the contents, the menu, and the EPUB gave it one. The number goes in where the page says its title, and the page, written with `--ascii`, has such a character as a reference ("Newton&#x2019;s Laws"), a formula as MathML, and a note as its mark, which the title, as text, didn't match: "Newton’s Laws" and "Limits a/b and ℝⁿ" had no number in either, and a heading with a note none in the heading. Each is read as text now, a formula as its text and a note's mark aside.
- **A title the source declares with a formula in it had the formula's TeX where a title is text**: Markdown's `title: The $x^2$ law` was "The x^2 law" in the page's `<title>`, the contents page, the menu, the pager, a cartridge's names, an EPUB chapter's title and the EPUB's contents page, a split page's pieces' source title, and the name of a book of one page; and a Word file's title had it as TeX, or, where the page's heading says the title, had lost it ("The  law again"). Each has the formula's text now, "The x² law", as a title made from a heading has (`bin/title-formulas.lua` notes a declared title's formulas too); the title keeps its formula where it's shown, in the HTML's title block and a Markdown copy's front matter. A formula that can't be text is its TeX between dollar signs, and the run's note says it's in a title. [Formats](docs/formats.md#a-pages-title).
- **A book's heading for a page lost its formulas where the book made it of the title's text**: an EPUB's numbered heading, "1.1 Choosing $\binom{n}{k}$ and X^(–)", and the EPUB's and the PDF's heading for a page with no title heading of its own, whose declared title the book set as its text, a formula's TeX and all, `\chapter{The x\^{}2 law}`. Each is the page's own words now, its formulas, notes, and emphasis among them, a numbered EPUB's with the number before them (`lib/bookassembly.py`), as an unnumbered book's heading for a page with a title heading was. The EPUB's contents has the formula as MathML there too, and a chapter's `<title>` as its text, as before.
- **The pager named the contents page by its file**, "Previous: toc" on the page after it, where the menu says "Contents": a page the run writes has its title in the contents alone. The pager takes a page's title from the contents where it gives one, as the menu does, so a page the contents renames is named as it's renamed there too.
- **A page that keeps the HTML title block, for a subtitle, a date, or an abstract, showed a title made from its heading with the heading's formulas as text**, `<h1 class="title">Limits a/b and more</h1>`, the heading itself not written there. The block's `<h1>` is the heading now where the title is its words, the formula as MathML, as the page's own heading has it elsewhere, and `<title>` has the text; a declared title the heading says more than stands there as written. [Formats](docs/formats.md#a-pages-title).
- **A deck's title with a formula in it had the formula's TeX in its files' titles**, "The x^2 law" for `title: The $x^2$ law` in its reveal.js presentation's `<title>`, its PDF's properties, and its PowerPoint file's, where its pages had the formula's text already. Each has the text now, "The x² law" (`target-blocks.lua` gives it the presentation as `pagetitle` and the PDF and the PowerPoint file as `title-meta`); the title slide keeps the formula. [Slides](docs/slides.md#decks-written-in-markdown).
- **A slide's title with a formula in it had the formula's TeX beside its text in the deck PDF's structure**, "A slide on $x^2$x²" for `## A slide on $x^2$`: ltx-talk titles a slide's heading in the structure with its title made plain (`\text_purify:n`), which kept both arguments of the `\texorpdfstring` the formula is set in for the slide's bookmark. The title is the bookmark's now, "A slide on x²". [Slides](docs/slides.md#the-pdf).
- **A face named with an underscore in its file's name, or in the folder its `Path` names, stopped LaTeX.** Pandoc's writer escapes a metadata field's text for LaTeX, a face's name and options too, so `mainfontoptions: [Path=fonts/Open_Sans/static/]`, a folder of the kind a downloaded face often comes in, was `Path=fonts/Open\_Sans/static/` in the template's `\setmainfont`, and LaTeX stopped there ("Missing \begin{document}", and "Missing \endcsname inserted" for a file so named). The fields the template writes into fontspec's commands, a face's name, its options, and its fallbacks, are written as the metadata file gives them now, a brace in an option too, which had been `\{`; and an apostrophe, two or three hyphens, and three periods are as typed, where Pandoc's reading of the file had made them typographer's marks, in the faces the build sets again too. The PDF suite's check of its CFF2 face's book had failed now and then for it, wherever the suite's folder had an underscore in its random name. [Configuration](docs/configuration.md#the-faces).
- **A Word source's copy repaired text in an equation that its page left as text.** `format: source` gave literal text (`m:lit`), which Pandoc reads as `\text{}`, what math gets, a middle dot the dot operator; and made a bar of a macron or an en dash typed as text and set over a letter, and a zero of a subscript's slashed O typed as text, which the page has as `\overset{\text{–}}{x}` and `H_{\text{Ø}}`. Each is as the page has it now. [Math](docs/configuration.md#math).
- **An EPUB reported a picture on another server and a link to a local file that it left out**, in a passage for another target (`::: {targets="web"}`), in `fidelity.csv`: Pandoc gave each image and link to the target filter's function before the div or span around it. A passage for another target is gone now before anything is made of what it holds.
- **A formula in a picture's description was its TeX in the picture's alt text**, "A square of side x^2" for `![A square of side $x^2$](square.png)`, in a page's, an EPUB's, and a reveal.js presentation's `alt`, a PDF's `/Alt`, a Word file's and a PowerPoint file's description, and AsciiDoc's `alt`, which a screen reader read as written. Each has the formula's text now, "A square of side x²", as a title has it (`target-blocks.lua`), and so does an EPUB's link for a picture on another server; a formula that can't be text, a matrix say, is its TeX between dollar signs. A Markdown copy keeps the formula. A Word file and an AsciiDoc file, whose alt text holds text alone, report each such picture in `fidelity.csv` (`alt-formula`). A figure's caption that repeats the alt text, which Pandoc's HTML writer hides from a screen reader, was shown beside it once the two differed, so its words are hidden in a span instead, but for a caption with a link or a note; a PowerPoint file marks such a caption decorative, as it does one with no formula. [Formats](docs/formats.md#a-pictures-alt-text).
- **An EPUB read a caption twice beside the link it makes of a picture on another server**: the EPUB makes such a picture a link named by its alt text, beside which Pandoc's writer hides no caption, where it hides one that repeats a picture's alt text, so a screen reader read the link and then the caption, the same words. A caption that says what the link says is hidden in a span now, as a picture's is, a formula in it or not (`target-blocks.lua`); a caption of its own is shown, and so is one beside an author's own link in a figure. [Formats](docs/formats.md#a-pictures-alt-text).
- **The run says once which formulas in pictures' descriptions are their TeX in the alt text**, a matrix or a binomial, which can't be text, as it says of a title's: "NOTE: 1 formula(s) in pictures' descriptions couldn't be written as text, as the others are, and are their TeX in the pictures' alt text, every format's but a Markdown copy's, which keeps the formula: \binom{n}{k}." (`title-formulas.lua`, `convert.py`). A run whose only target is a Markdown copy says nothing, and no run says it of a picture only a Markdown copy has, in a passage or a page's variant for it.
- **A LaTeX book's tagged copy could hyphenate it as another variety of its language.** babel takes its main language from `\DocumentMetadata`'s `lang` when the book doesn't name one with `main=`, so a German book in `ngerman`, the 1996 spelling, given `lang=de` was set in babel's `german`, by the 1901 rules ("Zucker" unbroken where the author's build has "Zu-cker"), and a British book in a project in `en-US` in `american` ("or-ga-ni-za-tion" for "or-gan-iz-a-tion"), in its tagged copy and its PDF from its own files. Where the copy gives the book its `lang`, it passes babel `main=` before the class, the language the book's own build makes babel's main one, and the PDF still declares the `lang`. A book that names its main language, or whose own `\DocumentMetadata` names one, is left as it is, and the run names one whose main language its options don't say. And a book whose babel options name a main language (`\usepackage[main=french,english]{babel}`) is read as in it, French here, where the reading took the last language, English, for the book's.
- **A LaTeX book stopped the reader with babel's `\foreignlanguage[date]`, or with `\foreignlanguage` whose words hold a paragraph break or a list** ("Nothing was converted"), and babeltags' `\textfr{...}` was dropped from the pages with its words. The reading's copy leaves out the option, makes words with a paragraph break (a blank line or `\par`, but not a blank line after `\\`, which the reader reads as a line break, nor `\\par`, a line break and a word) a `\foreignlanguage` in each paragraph, so each paragraph keeps the words before and after them, reads words holding a list or another environment the reader doesn't take inside a paragraph (any but a formula's, breqn's among them, one the book defines around a formula, or one it defines as text or copies from one) as LaTeX sets a list, a `\foreignlanguage` of the words before the environment ending their paragraph, the environment in an `otherlanguage`, and a `\foreignlanguage` of the words after it beginning the next, an environment the book defines with a list or a paragraph break in its code among them, and reads `\textfr{...}` as `\foreignlanguage{french}{...}`, for each tag the book's `\babeltags` declares and doesn't define again in a form the reader reads (`\renewcommand`, `\def`, `\RenewDocumentCommand`, and the rest); the author's files are as they were.
- **A space after an environment a LaTeX book defines was lost from its pages**, "Un motrouge ici." for `Un \begin{rouge}mot\end{rouge} rouge ici.`, and so was one after its beginning where it takes no arguments. The reading's copy writes such an environment as the commands LaTeX makes of it, and the reader took the space after a command's name with it, as TeX does. Where a space follows, each now ends with an empty group (`\endrouge{}`), so the space is kept, as LaTeX keeps it. Elsewhere it's as it was: before an environment's arguments, where LaTeX passes over a space too, before a group or an option that an opening code ending in a command may take for an argument (`\newenvironment{mytab}{\begin{tabular}}{\end{tabular}}`, its columns given after `\begin{mytab}`), and in a formula, one of the book's environments for formulas among them, where an empty group would be a superscript's base. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book stopped the reader with a paragraph break in a box's or a quotation's words** ("Nothing was converted"): a blank line or `\par` in `\mbox`, `\hbox`, `\underline`, ulem's `\uline` or `\sout`, or csquotes' `\enquote`, `\foreignquote`, or `\hyphenquote`, starred or not. The reading's copy sets a box's words on one line, as LaTeX does, a blank line as a space and `\par` as nothing, and makes a quotation's words a quotation in each paragraph, the nearest the pages come to LaTeX's one quotation over two, but in a box's words, where the box's line keeps it one quotation, and a paragraph of what the reader reads as nothing, a `\vspace`, gets no quotation marks of its own; the author's files are as they were. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book stopped the reader with a list or another environment in an inline command's words** ("Nothing was converted"), `\textbf`'s, `\emph`'s, `\href`'s, a quotation's, or `\mbox`'s (a tabular), which LaTeX builds. The reading's copy sets the environment between the command around the words before it and around those after it, as LaTeX sets a list, and a tabular in a box, which LaTeX sets in the line, as near as the pages come, and so a call of one of the book's own commands that makes one (`\newcommand{\vocab}[1]{\begin{tabular}{ll}#1\end{tabular}}`); the environment loses the command's formatting, which the pages can't give a block, but for a quotation's language, and the run names each place by its file and line. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book's passages in babel's `otherlanguage*` and babeltags' environment (`\begin{fr}`) had no language in its pages**, and `otherlanguage*`'s printed its language's name before it ("ngerman Ein Wort"). The reading's copy writes each as `\foreignlanguage`, which babel's `otherlanguage*` is as an environment, a paragraph break or a list in it read as in `\foreignlanguage`'s words; as `otherlanguage` where it holds a heading, code, `\input`, or another block the reader reads only as one, which it stops on in `\foreignlanguage`'s words, one of the book's own commands that makes one among them, or in a macro's code its argument, which may be one; and in a list, each item's words and a description's term, where the reader read them as paragraphs, out of the list; unless the book defines the tag's environment or its declaration again itself. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **When Pandoc's reader stopped on a LaTeX book's master, its error named a line past the author's**, "line 8" for line 7 of `book.tex`: Pandoc reads a copy, which puts its definition of `\centerline`, and the file of definitions for reading, before `\begin{document}`. The run now names the author's line too, matching the copy's lines to the author's file, a chapter's too where a repair moved its lines, and where that line is the master's `\begin{document}` or an `\include`, which Pandoc names for some stops in the first paragraphs of a file the master reads, says the stop may be in one. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#how-a-book-is-read).
- **A LaTeX book whose babel `main=` has a modifier (`main=ngerman.foo`) was set in another language without a word**: babel takes the value whole, loads no language for it, and sets the last it loaded as its main one. The run now warns of it, whether `\usepackage`, `\RequirePackage`, or `\PassOptionsToPackage` gives babel the `main=` it takes, the first it's given, and says to give the language with its modifier last among babel's options instead. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#the-source-target).
- **A LaTeX book's inline code changed in its pages, or stopped the reader**, listings' `\lstinline`, minted's `\mintinline`, and fancyvrb's `\Verb`: the reading's copy left `\verb` as it was but took these for LaTeX, so `\lstinline|\begin{rouge}x\end{rouge}|` showed `\rouge x\endrouge ` for an environment the book defines, and `\lstinline|\input chapitre|` stopped the reader ("Nothing was converted"). Each is left as it is now, to its end on its line, as the reader reads it. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **The LaTeX suite failed its checks of a book in German or in British English where TeX lacks their packages**, where the testing page says such a check skips: a book in `ngerman` stopped LuaLaTeX without `babel-german` ("Unknown option 'ngerman'"), and one in `british` was hyphenated by the American patterns without `hyphen-english`'s British ones ("Hyphen rules for 'british' set to \l@english"), so two of the checks of babel's main language failed, and nine of a book's passages babel sets the language of, as in Ubuntu 26.10's TeX Live 2026 without its German packages (CNX-NOTES, item 185 and What Round 53 left open). The suite names `babel-german`, `hyphen-german`, or `hyphen-english`, and the command that installs them, and those checks skip; the books' copies are checked all the same.
- **A LaTeX book's tagged copy tagged none of its babel passages under babel before 26.11, while the run said each was tagged.** babel 26.9, which Ubuntu 26.10's TeX Live 2026 has, runs the two hooks the copy's lines add to without declaring them, and LaTeX runs nothing in a hook that isn't declared (CNX-NOTES, item 185). The lines declare both first, where LaTeX can (`\ActivateGenericHook`), which changes nothing with a babel that declares them; a copy of the installed babel given 26.9's declarations tags them now, as the installed one does.
- **A tagged PDF's heading holding `\foreignlanguage` had the language's name in its title**: `\section{Le \foreignlanguage{french}{titre}}` was "Le frenchtitre" in the PDF's structure and in its contents entry's, where the bookmark had "Le titre", since LaTeX's tagging makes a title plain keeping the arguments of a command it doesn't know (CNX-NOTES, item 185). A tagged copy of a book that has one says what `\foreignlanguage` is in a plain title, its words alone, an option before its language taken with it.
- **A LaTeX book's space between words set in didot or cicero points was lost from its pages** (`\hspace{2dd}`), the words on either side running together: the reading took such a length for no space, since a second table of TeX's units, for rules and without those two, had replaced the first (`latexsource.POINTS`). One table has every unit TeX reads now, new didot and cicero among them, each as the TeXbook gives it, and the unit suite checks that no Python file binds a name twice at its top level.
- **A LaTeX book stopped the reader, or lost words from its pages, with an address, a box, soul's commands, or polyglossia's passages** ("Nothing was converted"), where LaTeX builds the book: an address between two of a character (`\url|...|`, `\nolinkurl!...!`), which in an `otherlanguage*` lost the passage's words instead, and a `%` in a braced one, which the reading's copy took for a comment, leaving an `otherlanguage*` holding it to the reader, its language's name printed ("french Voir ..."); url's `\path`, dropped with its words; a box's size, `\hbox to 3cm{...}`, read as words ("to 3cm boxed"), and a `\vbox` holding paragraphs or a list; a paragraph break in soul's `\ul`, `\st`, `\hl`, `\so`, or `\caps`, and `\so` and `\caps` themselves, dropped with their words; and polyglossia's `\textlang`, dropped with its words, and a list in `\textfrench`'s. The reading's copy braces such an address, takes a `%` in any address for no comment, as the reader does, and reads `\path` as `\nolinkurl`, code; leaves out a box's size, and reads such a `\vbox` as a minipage of the line's width, a division of its paragraphs; puts soul's command around each paragraph's words, as LaTeX sets them, and reads `\so` and `\caps` as their words, in small capitals for `\caps`; and reads `\textlang` as polyglossia's command for its language (`\textfrench`), its options with it, and a paragraph break or a list in its words, or a list in `\textfrench`'s, as `\foreignlanguage`'s. A paragraph break in `\textfrench`'s words, which LaTeX stops on too, is left. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A LaTeX book stopped the reader with a call of its own command that makes a block in an inline command's words, where the command was defined with `\def` and delimited arguments, `\DeclareRobustCommand`, or xparse's `\NewDocumentCommand`, or the block was an environment of its own that calls such a command** ("Nothing was converted"), as the reading's copy set apart only `\newcommand`'s and plain `\def`'s calls, as LaTeX sets them. The copy reads each such definition now, a `\def`'s call by its delimiters (`\def\vocab#1;{...}`, called as `\vocab a & b;`), and an environment whose code calls one is a block. So are a `\def`'s image macro and one that `\include`-s a chapter, which were read only with plain arguments: the chapters a book `\include`-s through `\def\chap#1;{\include{#1}}` had no pages; and the source target writes a description into a delimited argument braced once more, as TeX takes a pair of braces off it. With them, an environment of the book's whose codes open and close `otherlanguage*` (`\newenvironment{allemand}{\begin{otherlanguage*}{ngerman}}{\end{otherlanguage*}}`), or that takes the language and gives it to `otherlanguage*`, which the reader expanded to one, printing the language's name and giving the passage none, is read in its language; the items a command of the book's makes (`\newcommand{\q}[1]{\item #1}`) in an `otherlanguage*` in a list, which the reader read out of the list, are the list's, in the language; one of the book's environments in the code of a command a formula uses (`$\foo^2$`) no longer gives the superscript an empty base; the book's own `\Verb`, `\lstinline`, or `\mintinline`, which every repair took for inline code, is read as the book defines it, and one it gives only with `\providecommand` beside the package that defines it, loaded by the book, a package of its own, or tcolorbox's libraries, which LaTeX doesn't take and the reader did, is left out, so the package's is read; a comment that opens a piece the copy cuts goes before its command; babel's `main=` with a modifier is warned of where a file the preamble `\input`-s or a package of the book's own gives it, and the warning names that file; a file the book writes itself with `filecontents`, which the reader reads an `\input` of, isn't taken for one the book's build makes, which stopped the run ("reaches 1 file(s) that aren't here"); and the copy of a long chapter is made faster: its passages were cut in 2.0 seconds on a chapter of a megabyte with 45,000 inline commands and 64 of the book's commands that make a block, and are cut in 0.9. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A quotation csquotes sets in a LaTeX book had English marks in its pages, where LaTeX sets another language's**: Pandoc's writers write a quotation's marks as “ ” whatever the page's language, so a French book's `\enquote{un}`, « un » in its own PDF, was “un”. The reading's copy writes the marks LaTeX sets it with, csquotes' own for the book's main language, or with `autostyle` for the language around each quotation, an inner quotation's too, in the variant csquotes' options give a language (`german=guillemets`), the options read in order, as csquotes reads them (`style=german,autostyle=once` is the main language's style). A book whose quotations LaTeX sets in English's marks is read as before. Not read: a `\setquotestyle` in the document, and with `autostyle` a `\selectlanguage` in an earlier file. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **A list in `\textcolor`'s words in a LaTeX book's paragraph ended the paragraph before the words**: Pandoc's reader reads `\textcolor` around a list as a colored division, so the colored words before the list were a paragraph of their own. The reading's copy cuts the words there, `\textcolor` around the words before the list, which end their paragraph, around the list, and around the words after it, which begin the next, as LaTeX colors them; and in another command's words, `\textbf{gras \textcolor{red}{a \begin{itemize}...}}`, where it stopped the reader ("Nothing was converted"), the colored list is a block that command's words are cut at. [LaTeX sources](docs/latex.md#what-is-put-right-before-reading).
- **Where Pandoc named a LaTeX book's master's line for a stop in a file the master reads, the run said only that the stop might be in one of them**: Pandoc names the master's `\begin{document}` or `\include` for some stops in a file's first paragraphs. The run now reads each file the document reads alone, after the copy's preamble, and names the first that stops and its line.
- **A LaTeX book whose main language babel's `\babelprovide` sets was read in its options' language**: `\usepackage[english]{babel}` with `\babelprovide[import,main]{french}`, which babel makes French, gave the book's pages `lang="en"`, which a screen reader picks its voice by, its tagged copy `lang=en`, and its quotations English's marks. The last `\babelprovide` with `main` among its options names the book's main language now, in its pages, its copy, and its quotations, by the tag its `import=` gives (`import=es-MX`), else by its name. [Sidecars](docs/sidecars.md#a-pages-language).
- **A footnote's mark in a definition list's term was drawn against the definition's first word in a PDF**, a book's or a deck's: the note is set at the head of the definition, since LaTeX's tagging stops on one in a list's label, and its mark came after the space LaTeX sets after a term, "Elasticity ¹How much".
